2 Compute Infrastructure

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why does infrastructure, not algorithms alone, determine who can participate in advancing machine learning at scale?

Machine learning systems have a physical reality beyond their code. Public algorithms do not make large-scale training equally accessible: executing them requires power, cooling, memory, and communication capacity that organizations must build and sustain. A model that outgrows one machine turns a local resource problem into a shared infrastructure commitment. Buying more accelerators adds arithmetic capacity, but it also adds data transfers, heat, and components whose failures can interrupt collective work. The useful capacity of the fleet depends on whether those demands can be met together. Hardware that appears attractive in isolation may spend much of its deployment waiting for data or operating below its advertised speed. A facility that can house equipment may lack the electrical or cooling capacity to run it at the intended density. These mismatches consume both capital and operating budgets while reducing the experiments that can finish on schedule. Infrastructure decisions also outlast individual models, so an organization must weigh the efficiency of specialization against the flexibility needed for future workloads. Single-machine performance remains part of that judgment, but multiplying it by the number of machines cannot establish what the complete installation will deliver. Participation at scale therefore depends on turning purchased hardware into sustained, usable capacity within physical and financial limits. In C³ terms, the physical substrate sets the compute and communication bounds within which the fleet must coordinate useful work.

Learning Objectives
  • Compare GPU, TPU, and ASIC dataflows to explain specialization across the accelerator spectrum
  • Apply roofline analysis to distinguish compute-bound from memory-bandwidth-bound phases of training and inference
  • Calculate token latency or memory budgets from accelerator memory capacity, bandwidth, precision, and model state size
  • Match tensor, pipeline, and data parallelism to the available interconnect bandwidth hierarchy
  • Evaluate rack and pod designs using power density, cooling limits, topology, and facility reliability constraints
  • Select accelerator infrastructure using sustained throughput, power constraints, workload fit, and deployment cost

2.1 The Infrastructure Walls

The scaling laws of AI Scaling Laws are why demand outruns any single device. Loss falls predictably as parameters, training tokens, and compute grow together, so each step up in capability multiplies both the memory a model occupies and the arithmetic its training consumes; a GPT-3-class model already needs several accelerators merely to store its FP16 weights. A model that exceeds one accelerator’s memory capacity forces a hardware decision before training begins. Adding accelerators supplies capacity, but also creates transfers between devices, increases power demand, and couples progress to more components that can fail. The familiar single-node compute and memory limits therefore become inputs to fleet design. Figure 1 maps these four recurring physical constraints across escalating physical scales, from an eight-GPU node to a 25K-accelerator fleet. Memory bandwidth, rack power, pod communication, and fleet failure exposure provide the examples; the workload and configuration determine which constraint binds at each scale. These are the memory, energy, and network walls of the scale moment and the fleet’s reliability gap, seen from the hardware that imposes them. Because this chapter studies the hardware, it names each constraint by the physical resource that runs out, and it uses these four names from here on: the memory wall, the power wall (the energy wall as power delivery and heat removal), the communication wall (the network wall as link bandwidth), and the reliability wall (the reliability gap as a failure interval).

The reliability wall starts from a per-GPU failure figure that The Scale Moment calls an MTBF. A failed GPU is replaced rather than repaired, so this chapter calls the per-device figure a mean time to failure (MTTF) and reserves mean time between failures (MTBF) for nodes and fleets, which keep running through repeated failures and replacements. The number is the same; only the label records what is being counted.

↩︎ The memory, network, and energy walls of the scale moment are established in The Scale Moment.

The 175B model anchors this progression. It is the chapter’s running instance of Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3), a GPT-3-class dense transformer trained with mixed precision and the Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) optimizer, whose reference training cluster is 1,024 H100 GPUs in 128 eight-GPU nodes. Accelerator selection establishes the compute rate, memory capacity, and power envelope that a fleet can buy. The node must connect enough of those accelerators to hold and execute the workload; the rack must power and cool them; the pod must sustain communication and survive component failures. This is the hardware contribution to C³: useful fleet compute depends on the communication and coordination costs created at each physical boundary.

↩︎ The three fleet workload archetypes are established in Three systems archetypes.

↪ The running model’s parameters and reference cluster are developed in Reference Workload.

The chapter climbs the same scale ladder, so the four walls arrive in the order the figure reads, though not one wall per section. The memory wall begins at the accelerator and is developed in section 2.3. The communication wall first appears inside the node, where section 2.5.1 measures the cliff between local and inter-node links, and returns at pod scale in section 2.7. The power wall binds at the rack (section 2.6.1), and the reliability wall accumulates from node health (section 2.5.5) through facility controls to the fleet-scale failure interval of section 2.7.1.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Which statement correctly describes representative constraints and mitigation mechanisms across the accelerator, node, rack, and pod hierarchy, without assigning one universally dominant wall to each tier?

    1. Accelerator: arithmetic throughput vs. generality tax \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by specialized silicon; Node: single-chip memory capacity limit \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by high-speed intra-node fabrics aggregating HBM; Rack: thermal and power density \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by facility cooling and power distribution; Pod: network bisection and fleet MTBF \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by pod-scale fabrics and fault-tolerant orchestration.
    2. Accelerator: network latency \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by InfiniBand; Node: transistor count \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by photolithography; Rack: memory bandwidth \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by PCIe Gen5; Pod: power delivery \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by running jobs asynchronously without barriers.
    3. Accelerator: single-chip memory capacity wall \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by on-die branch predictors; Node: power wall \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by host underclocking; Rack: communication wall \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by standard commodity Ethernet; Pod: memory bandwidth wall \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by building-wide CXL pooling.
    4. Accelerator: storage I/O capacity \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by local NVMe drives; Node: compilation time \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by JIT compilers; Rack: clock jitter \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by atomic clocks; Pod: software bug rate \(\rightarrow\) mitigated by unit testing frameworks.

  2. Explain how relieving a constraint at one physical level of the compute infrastructure stack can expose another constraint. Use the accelerator, node, rack, and pod hierarchy, and explain why it is not a fixed one-wall-per-tier sequence.

  3. True or False: In a warehouse-scale training cluster, the reliability wall implies that as the cluster scales to tens of thousands of GPUs, the aggregate fleet Mean Time Between Failures (MTBF) remains bounded primarily by the lifetime of the most resilient server chassis.

See Answers →

2.2 The Accelerator

The progression begins with the accelerator, because every wall above it is measured against the rates one device can compute, store, and draw. An accelerator purchase commits the fleet to a workload and software stack for longer than one experiment. A research group changing model operations frequently needs programmability; a service executing a stable computation at sustained volume can justify more specialization. The familiar trade-off between control flexibility and arithmetic efficiency matters here because an architectural mismatch is replicated across the fleet. Comparing programmable and specialized accelerators therefore starts with how much of the workload and compiler stack can remain stable over the deployment’s lifetime.

Systems Perspective 2.1: The generality tax
General-purpose CPUs allocate silicon to caches and control logic as well as arithmetic. Branch predictors, reorder buffers, and instruction decoders support code with irregular control flow and data access. Matrix multiplication has a more regular schedule, so some of that flexibility contributes little to its dominant computation. The generality tax (principle 1) is the area and energy devoted to capabilities outside the dominant workload.

Specialized accelerators reduce this tax by replacing general-purpose control with a dataflow matched to the workload. In the approximate shares of figure 2, the arithmetic fraction of the die rises from roughly 5–10 percent on a CPU to 50–60 percent on a GPU, 70–80 percent on a Tensor Processing Unit (TPU), and over 90 percent on a custom application-specific integrated circuit (ASIC). The shares mark a direction rather than measured proportions for any product. The selection question is how much of the complete workload can use the specialized hardware.

The transition from general-purpose to specialized silicon follows a logical progression. At one end of the spectrum, GPUs retain substantial programmability while providing 10–100\(\times\) the matrix throughput of CPUs. At the other end, custom ASICs hardwire a specific dataflow for maximum efficiency at the cost of flexibility.

Understanding where each architecture falls on this spectrum, and why, is essential for selecting hardware that matches a given workload. The spectrum is not a ranking from “bad” (general-purpose CPU) to “good” (custom ASIC), but a continuum of trade-offs where each position offers the best match for a different combination of workload characteristics and deployment constraints.

GPUs represent the first major step away from general-purpose computing toward massive parallelism while retaining programmability. An NVIDIA H100, for example, contains 16,896 CUDA cores organized into 132 streaming multiprocessors (SMs). Each SM can keep many threads resident under the single instruction, multiple threads (SIMT)1 execution model, but only a subset issues instructions in a given cycle. Unlike a CPU, which optimizes for single-thread latency, a GPU hides memory latency by maintaining thousands of threads in flight and switching between them when one stalls on a memory access.

1 SIMT (single instruction, multiple threads): NVIDIA coined the term to distinguish its model from classical single instruction, multiple data (SIMD): each thread keeps its own registers and may branch independently, and the hardware, not the programmer, masks lanes when a warp diverges. That convenience has a price on irregular ML operations such as sparse attention or dynamic expert routing, where masked lanes sit idle while the other path executes.

The programmer writes a single function (a kernel), and the hardware maps it across a massive grid of threads. This model is flexible enough to run any mathematical kernel, from convolutions to attention mechanisms to custom loss functions, while providing orders of magnitude more throughput than a CPU for data-parallel computation.

The trade-off is that irregular, branch-heavy code runs poorly because divergent threads within a warp (a group of 32 threads that execute in lockstep) must execute serially. When threads in the same warp take different branches of an if-else statement, the hardware must execute both branches sequentially, disabling the threads that took the other path during each branch. For ML workloads, this divergence penalty is usually small because neural network operations have highly uniform control flow: every element in a matrix multiplication follows the same computation path.

TPUs specialize the matrix dataflow while retaining other units for operations that do not map onto it. Google’s Tensor Processing Units use a systolic array2 architecture: a fixed grid of multiply-accumulate (MAC) units where data flows between neighboring cells in a regular, wave-like pattern. Instead of fetching and decoding a separate instruction for each MAC, the array passes operands and partial results between cells on a regular schedule. Local reuse reduces instruction and memory traffic within the matrix computation. The complete accelerator still needs control, data movement, and execution of nonmatrix operations.

2 Systolic array: From Greek systole (contraction), the name describes data pulsing through a grid of processing elements in a heart-like rhythm. Kung and Leiserson (1979) proposed the design for VLSI chips whose limiting resource was memory access rather than logic (Kung 1982): each operand fetched once is reused by every cell it passes. That ratio of computation to memory traffic is exactly what matrix multiplication at ML scale rewards.

Kung, Hsiang Tsung, and Charles E Leiserson. 1979. “Systolic Arrays (for VLSI).” Sparse Matrix Proceedings 1978 1: 256–82.
Kung, H. T. 1982. “Why Systolic Architectures?” Computer 15 (1): 37–46. https://doi.org/10.1109/mc.1982.1653825.

The cost of this efficiency is reduced programmability. Models must be compiled through Accelerated Linear Algebra (XLA), which maps high-level operations onto the fixed dataflow. Workloads with irregular control flow or dynamic shapes may not map well to this architecture, and the compilation process itself can be time-consuming (minutes to hours for complex models), which slows the iteration cycle during model development.

The programming model distinction between GPUs and TPUs has practical implications for organizational decisions. A research team that frequently adds custom operations, such as new attention patterns or activation functions, needs a path to implement them as kernels without waiting for compiler support, and must check how each platform expresses and optimizes those operations. A team running a stable computation at scale can place more weight on sustained utilization and compilation cost amortized over many runs. CUDA kernel support and XLA graph compilation offer different development paths, but neither establishes a utilization advantage without evaluating the workload and implementation. Software fit belongs in the hardware comparison because unsupported or poorly mapped work can leave specialized arithmetic idle.

Example 2.1: The TPU origin story
Scenario: In 2013, Google projected that if users spoke to Android phones for 3 minutes per day using voice search, handling the inference load on general-purpose CPUs would require doubling data center compute capacity at prohibitive cost (Jouppi et al. 2017).

Diagnosis: Standard CPU architectures paid a heavy generality tax, spending significant silicon area and power on out-of-order execution, branch prediction, and complex caches rather than matrix multiplication throughput.

Systems lesson: Hardware specialization is economically justified when a workload is massive, stable, and dominated by a single mathematical kernel (MatMul). The TPU v1 hardwired a \(256 \times 256\) systolic array to deliver 92 TOPS (INT8) within a 75 W footprint, establishing custom ASIC accelerators in production serving infrastructure.

Jouppi, Norman P., Cliff Young, Nishant Patil, David Patterson, Gaurav Agrawal, Raminder Bajwa, Sarah Bates, et al. 2017. “In-Datacenter Performance Analysis of a Tensor Processing Unit.” Proceedings of the 44th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, ISCA ’17, 1–12. https://doi.org/10.1145/3079856.3080246.

Custom ASICs occupy the specialized end of the spectrum, where the economics of silicon can justify giving up much of the general-purpose programmability. When an organization runs a stable workload at enormous scale, designing a chip specifically for that workload may become economically rational. A custom ASIC can improve energy per operation and sustained utilization on its target workload by removing features that computation does not require.

Tesla’s Dojo D1 chip, for example, is optimized for the spatial and temporal structure of video-based vision models. Its hundreds of tightly coupled processing nodes are designed around the dataflow needs of Tesla’s vision pipeline, with on-chip SRAM sized to keep spatial tiles close to the compute units. This reduces the repeated round-trips to off-chip memory that a general-purpose GPU would require for the same computation.

AWS Trainium takes a different approach, targeting the broad category of Transformer training rather than a single model. Trainium systems combine compiler-managed memory scheduling with hardware-assisted collective communication, so common training patterns such as tensor-parallel synchronization and data-parallel reductions can be optimized in the accelerator fabric rather than handled entirely by host software.

The risk of custom silicon follows from the same specialization. A change in the dominant model architecture can reduce the value of a fixed dataflow if the new operators, tensor shapes, or memory demands map poorly onto it. Sharing matrix operations across model families can preserve some of that investment, so a change from convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to Transformers does not by itself make an accelerator unusable. The forecasting window is long: a new ASIC typically takes 2–3 years from conception to deployment and then serves for several more, so the design decision must anticipate which operations will remain useful years after the workload was profiled, and how much flexibility is needed for the rest. In 2015, few would have predicted that attention-based Transformers would replace CNNs as the dominant architecture within five years, a span of only about two ASIC design cycles.

Custom silicon is therefore a bet on workload stability, and the organizations that make this bet are typically those with enough scale to justify the $50–$200 million development cost and enough workload volume to amortize the per-chip NRE (nonrecurring engineering) cost across millions of chips. Google can justify the TPU’s development cost because high-volume services such as Search, YouTube recommendations, and Gmail spam filtering can amortize a shared accelerator platform. A research lab running a few hundred GPUs cannot justify the same investment.

The bet can pay off when workloads are stable: a purpose-built ASIC can deliver 5–10\(\times\) the energy efficiency of a general-purpose GPU for its target operation. A workload shift can strand that advantage because the chip’s fixed dataflow may not accommodate a substantially different computational pattern. Pushing the locality argument further raises a question of physical scale: whether the compute fabric can expand to the size of an entire wafer.

2.2.1 Wafer-scale engines

Wafer-scale engines (WSE) pursue data locality across most of a silicon wafer. Unlike architectures built from chiplets or discrete dies connected by package- or board-level links, a wafer-scale engine such as the Cerebras WSE-3 uses a continuous compute fabric the size of a dinner plate. It can maintain an on-wafer interconnect across that surface because the wafer is not diced into individual chips.

For its 2024 WSE-3 configuration, Cerebras reported 900,000 AI-optimized cores, 44 GB of distributed on-chip SRAM, and 21 PB/s of aggregate memory bandwidth in its registration filing (Cerebras Systems 2024). These specifications are not directly comparable to GPU-cluster peak FLOP/s or high-bandwidth memory (HBM) bandwidth because the architectures expose different dataflows, memory organizations, precisions, and workload mappings. The durable comparison is topological: on-wafer links avoid package-, board-, and rack-level transfers for work that maps onto the fabric.

Cerebras Systems. 2024. Cerebras Systems, Inc. Registration Statement on Form S-1.
Systems, Cerebras. 2021. “Wafer-Scale Deep Learning Acceleration with the Cerebras CS-2.” Cerebras Technical Paper 2021.

Figure 3 contrasts the package and board boundaries of a multi-GPU chassis with wafer-scale integration. The eight GPUs exchange state within the chassis through NVSwitch; transfers to other nodes cross the external network. A wafer-scale engine instead routes between 900,000 on-wafer cores on the 300 mm wafer. Defect-aware routing lets the fabric use working regions despite local manufacturing flaws. In the documented CS-2 configuration, twelve power supplies in a 9+3 redundant arrangement could deliver up to 23 kW to the WSE-2 (Systems 2021). That configuration-specific design illustrates why wafer-scale power delivery and cooling require system-level liquid-cooling hardware rather than a conventional accelerator heatsink.

Wafer-scale engines specialize the physical architecture while retaining flexibility in the computational model through programmable cores. Their scale-up approach replaces some package- and board-level transfers with on-wafer movement. Capacity and data injection still constrain which parts of a workload can benefit from that locality.

The wafer-scale trade-off exchanges manufacturing complexity and defect-aware routing for an on-wafer fabric that avoids package- and board-level communication between its 900,000 cores. Where the wafer-scale engine removes the package boundary by never dicing the wafer, chiplet designs keep the package and change what goes inside it.

2.2.2 Chiplet-based accelerators

A major accelerator design pattern is the chiplet architecture, exemplified by NVIDIA’s Blackwell and AMD’s Instinct MI300 series. Rather than fabricating a single monolithic die, chiplet-based designs partition the processor into multiple smaller dies connected by a high-bandwidth die-to-die interconnect on a common package substrate. This approach addresses two physical limitations that constrain monolithic designs.

First, the maximum die size is capped by the photolithographic reticle limit, the largest area that a lithography scanner can pattern in a single exposure. The limit comes from the optics of photolithography, because the lens system of an extreme-ultraviolet (EUV) scanner projects a pattern onto a field of only about \(26{\times}33\) mm (858 mm²). A larger die would need multiple exposures stitched together, which is technically possible but sharply raises cost and lowers yield. The limit caps the number of Tensor Cores, SMs, and HBM stacks a monolithic GPU can integrate. Chiplet designs bypass this limit by placing multiple dies on a single package, with the B200’s two reticle-limited dies effectively creating a processor of about 1,700 mm².

Second, manufacturing yield depends on die area and defect density. Under the standard Poisson defect model, the fraction of defect-free dies falls exponentially with die area at a fixed defect density, because a single killer defect anywhere on the die can render it unusable. Smaller dies expose less area to each defect, and testing can select known-good chiplets before package assembly, so one defect discards a small die rather than a reticle-sized one. The resulting yield advantage must be weighed against the cost and yield of the package, its interconnects, and the additional testing. Chiplets change the manufacturing trade-off; they do not guarantee a lower cost per unit of compute.

The trade-off is the die-to-die interconnect. Communication between chiplets within a package is faster than communication between packages (NVLink) but slower than communication within a single monolithic die (the on-die mesh network). Workloads that generate frequent, fine-grained communication between processing elements (such as operations that share data between nonadjacent SMs) may experience a latency penalty when those SMs reside on different chiplets. GPU vendors mitigate this by making the die-to-die link transparent to the programmer, so the software sees a single logical GPU regardless of the underlying chiplet topology.

2.2.3 Evolution of GPU architectures

The rapid evolution of NVIDIA’s GPU architectures over the past decade illustrates how accelerator design has adapted to the changing demands of ML workloads. Each generation has introduced architectural innovations targeted at the specific bottlenecks revealed by production ML workloads, rather than simply increasing transistor counts.

The Volta architecture (2017) introduced the first-generation Tensor Core, a unit that executes a small matrix multiply-accumulate as a single instruction, recognizing that neural network training spends the majority of its time in matrix multiplications. Adding dedicated matrix-multiply-accumulate hardware alongside the existing CUDA cores allowed Volta to accelerate the dominant workload pattern without sacrificing the general-purpose programmability that made GPUs attractive for research. The V100, Volta’s flagship, delivered 125 TFLOP/s of FP16 Tensor Core throughput at 300 W.

The Ampere architecture (2020) expanded Tensor Core support to TF32 and BF16, after Turing had introduced INT8 and INT4 Tensor Core modes for quantized inference. The A100 also introduced Multi-Instance GPU (MIG), which partitions a single GPU into up to seven isolated instances, enabling efficient sharing of expensive hardware across multiple inference workloads. The most consequential change for infrastructure was Ampere’s third-generation NVLink, which doubled bidirectional bandwidth to 600 GB/s per GPU from Volta’s 300 GB/s, directly addressing the communication wall for tensor parallelism, which splits each layer’s matrices across GPUs.

The Hopper architecture (2022) added the Transformer Engine, whose FP8 recipes and autocast integration run supported Transformer operations in FP8 with managed scaling while retaining higher precision where the configured recipe or implementation requires it. Hopper also introduced NVLink 4.0 at 900 GB/s per GPU and the NVLink Switch, enabling NVLink connectivity beyond the 8-GPU boundary.

The H100’s 1,979 TFLOP/s of FP8 Tensor Core throughput at 700 W represents roughly 6.8× the V100’s peak FP16 throughput per watt of thermal design power (TDP). A factor of two of that gain comes from comparing FP8 with FP16; at equal FP16 precision the gain is 3.4×, which process technology (TSMC 4N) and architecture account for.

The Blackwell architecture (2024) continued this trajectory with the B200, which pairs two GPU dies in a single package via a high-bandwidth chip-to-chip link, effectively creating a “dual-die GPU” that delivers about 4,500 TFLOP/s of dense FP8 Tensor Core throughput at 1000 W. FP16/BF16 comparisons should use the lower precision-specific vendor figure rather than the FP8 peak.

The dual-die approach acknowledges that single-die GPU sizes have reached the 858 mm² reticle limit introduced with chiplets in section 2.2.2, so further scaling of one package requires the chiplet route rather than a larger die.

Blackwell also introduced fifth-generation NVLink at 1800 GB/s per GPU, doubling the intra-node bandwidth again. The die-to-die link within the B200 package operates at 10 TB/s, fast enough that the two dies can appear as a single logical GPU to the software for many operations.

The progression reveals a consistent pattern: each generation addresses a bottleneck exposed by the previous generation. Volta added dedicated matrix hardware for the compute bottleneck. Ampere expanded mixed-precision support. Hopper targeted Transformer-specific precision with FP8. Blackwell pushed against the die-size limit with multi-die packaging.

At each step, the accelerator’s design reflects an important workload of its era, illustrating how the physics of the workload drives the physics of the silicon. This co-evolution between workloads and hardware is not accidental: accelerator architects profile ML workloads to identify bottlenecks, and then design subsequent generations to address them. The result is a hardware evolution that is tightly coupled to model architecture evolution, which is why Transformer workloads have strongly shaped accelerator design since 2017.

The evolution also reveals a sobering procurement pattern for infrastructure planners: high-end accelerator generations can turn over faster than the facility that houses them. The V100 remained the flagship training GPU for approximately three years (2017–2020). The A100 held that position for roughly two years (2020–2022). Hopper and Blackwell continued that short-cadence pattern in the mid-2020s.

The planning implication is that a data center built for accelerators must anticipate refresh cycles before the first rack arrives. Table 1 connects those generations to changes in interconnect capacity and power. From V100 to B200, dense FP16 throughput rose 18\(\times\), memory bandwidth 8.9\(\times\), and TDP 3.3\(\times\) (figure 4). These are growth multiples over the whole span, not annual rates, and their ratios carry the planning lesson. Each byte delivered from memory must now feed about twice as many operations, and each watt of the power allowance supplies more than five times as many peak operations. The first ratio changes which workloads can approach peak throughput; the second, multiplied by device count, sets the proposed rack load. A facility refreshed only for compute density therefore outruns both its memory delivery and its cooling envelope.

Because table 1 mixes FP16 and FP8 peaks, its per-watt efficiency must be read at fixed precision. At dense FP16, dividing each generation’s peak throughput by its TDP gives a V100-to-B200 gain of 5.4× in peak throughput per watt, the more-than-fivefold figure above, against 18× in raw throughput; the rest of the raw gain came from a power allowance that itself rose 3.3×. Facilities must therefore accommodate higher device power as well as greater arithmetic capacity, which connects this trend to the power wall in section 2.6.1.

2.2.4 Evolution of TPU architectures

Google’s TPU trajectory follows a different path, focusing on distributed efficiency and XLA compiler integration. While GPUs emphasize peak per-chip throughput and software flexibility (CUDA), the TPU line has treated the pod as its unit of design since its second generation. From TPU v2 onward, every TPU chip is designed as part of a larger cluster, with dedicated inter-chip interconnect (ICI) links that bypass the host CPU entirely.

The generation sequence shows how that pod-first design moved from inference to training. The TPU v1 (2015) was a dedicated inference chip with a \(256{\times}256\) systolic array, delivering 92 TOPS (INT8) for the matrix operations that dominated Google’s production inference workloads. TPU v2 (2017) and TPU v3 (2018) then shifted the architecture toward training by adding bfloat16 (BF16), avoiding the dynamic range problems of standard FP16, and scaling the pod concept until a TPU v3 pod joined 1,024 chips with about 126 PFLOP/s of aggregate BF16 peak. TPU v4 (2021) extended the same logic into the network itself, adding an optical circuit switch so the physical topology could be reconfigured for different workloads, added SparseCore units that accelerate embedding lookups, and raised per-chip BF16 throughput to 275 TFLOP/s.

The TPU v5p (2023) continued this pod-scale design for high-end training. It features 459 TFLOP/s of BF16 compute, 95 GB of HBM2e with 2,760 GB/s memory bandwidth, and 1,200 GB/s bidirectional ICI bandwidth per chip. AllReduce combines values contributed by a group of accelerators, such as gradient sums, and returns the combined result to every participant. Its cost depends on the links connecting that group, which makes it a hardware-selection concern before the collective algorithms are studied. Table 2 shows how that pod-first design evolved across TPU generations. AllReduce develops the complexity model and algorithm choices needed to determine when those exchanges limit training throughput.

Set side by side, table 1 and table 2 show two design philosophies. GPU evolution packs more arithmetic, in ever lower-precision Tensor Core formats, onto one package, and turns to chiplets once a single die reaches its size limit. TPU evolution treats the pod as the product. Per-chip arithmetic matters, but inter-chip bandwidth and reconfigurability decide how much of it a large training job can use. Both lines combine local computation with memory and interconnect improvements; they place those resources differently. The warehouse-scale perspective of The shift to the warehouse-scale computer treats chip throughput, collective bandwidth, and reconfigurability as parts of one system (Barroso et al. 2019), so the useful comparison is how each candidate sustains the intended workload across that complete system. Table 3 collects the whole spectrum surveyed in this section, from CPU to custom ASIC, by its arithmetic core, execution model, and memory control.

For the 175B running model, the choice among these columns is not purely about peak FLOP/s. A research lab experimenting with novel architectures weekly can justify the GPU’s generality tax by its flexibility. An organization deploying a fixed Transformer at scale for years may find that the TPU’s dataflow efficiency or a custom ASIC’s power advantage dominates total cost. The accelerator spectrum is ultimately an economic question of how much flexibility can be surrendered, given the stability of the workload.

The H100’s arithmetic engine can reach 1,979 TFLOP/s in its advertised low-precision mode, but eligible operations approach that throughput only when data reaches the compute units fast enough. Accelerator selection therefore requires a second comparison, between the workload’s data demand and the memory system that feeds the arithmetic.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A hardware vendor transitions their next-generation accelerator from a monolithic \(800\text{ mm}^2\) die to a dual-die chiplet architecture (such as the NVIDIA B200 with an equivalent area of about \(1{,}700\text{ mm}^2\)) connected on a single package. What physical constraint is this transition primarily designed to bypass, and what trade-off does it introduce?

    1. It bypasses the memory wall by moving all memory on-chip, but introduces warp divergence because threads must synchronize across the die boundary.
    2. It bypasses the photolithographic reticle limit (\(\approx 858\text{ mm}^2\)) and improves manufacturing yield, but introduces a die-to-die interconnect that is slower than a continuous on-die mesh.
    3. It bypasses the maximum TDP limit of a single package by separating thermal loads into different server chassis, but requires developers to write separate CUDA kernels for each chiplet.
    4. It bypasses the communication wall by replacing electrical copper signaling with optical waveguides, but increases the generality tax of control logic.

  2. A research lab frequently experiments with novel, custom attention patterns and dynamic sparsity, while a production platform team trains a fixed standard Transformer at massive scale for months. Compare the systems trade-offs of choosing GPUs (CUDA) versus TPUs (XLA) for these two organizational teams.

  3. Comparing the V100 and B200 at dense FP16 reveals an ‘Accelerator Efficiency Wall.’ Which statement correctly captures the difference between peak throughput growth and throughput per TDP watt, and its infrastructure consequence?

    1. Peak throughput per TDP watt grew faster than peak throughput, allowing higher-density clusters to ignore power delivery.
    2. Both peak throughput and power efficiency scaled linearly at exactly the same rate, confirming that Dennard scaling remains fully active in modern 4nm nodes.
    3. Peak dense FP16 throughput grew about 18 times while peak throughput per TDP watt grew about 5.4 times, so facilities must accommodate higher device power as well as greater arithmetic capacity.
    4. Peak throughput plateaued after Volta (V100), meaning all recent performance gains stem exclusively from host CPU software optimizations.

  4. The area and energy devoted by a processor to capabilities outside the dominant neural network matrix-multiplication workload, such as out-of-order scheduling, branch prediction, and complex hardware caches, is called the ____.

  5. Arrange the following processor architectures in ascending order of the approximate percentage of their die area devoted strictly to arithmetic units (from lowest arithmetic die allocation to highest): (1) Google TPU (systolic array), (2) Purpose-built custom ASIC, (3) General-purpose server CPU, (4) NVIDIA GPU (with Tensor Cores).

See Answers →

2.3 The Memory Wall

As arithmetic throughput grows faster than data-access speed, compute units can spend more time waiting for operands. This divergence is known as the memory wall (Wulf and McKee 1995). While transistor scaling has increased logic performance by orders of magnitude, the interconnects that feed data to those cores have not kept pace. Neural networks can require large weight and activation streams, making bandwidth rather than compute the governing constraint for some inference and training regimes.

Wulf, Wm. A., and Sally A. McKee. 1995. “Hitting the Memory Wall: Implications of the Obvious.” ACM SIGARCH Computer Architecture News 23 (1): 20–24. https://doi.org/10.1145/216585.216588.

↪ The diagnostic table that maps memory-bound, compute-bound, and latency-bound symptoms to the optimizations that help is developed in Bottleneck diagnostic.

The implications are concrete in our running example. Our 175B model’s weights occupy 350 GB in FP16. In dense autoregressive decode at batch size 1, each generated token uses all of those weights, whose footprint far exceeds on-chip storage, so every token must stream them in from memory. Suppose, hypothetically, that one H100 could hold them. Streaming the whole 350 GB through that device’s HBM at about 3.35 TB/s takes over 100 ms per token, long enough to be a perceptible pause between generated words. No real H100 holds the model, so the figure is a floor on bandwidth demand rather than a prediction of deployed latency, but it shows that the memory wall is a delay a user can notice, not an abstract architectural ratio.

Three levers lower that floor, and the rest of this section develops each. Sharding spreads the weights across more memory paths, so each path streams fewer bytes. Quantization shrinks the bytes themselves. Reuse lets each fetched byte feed more arithmetic, which HBM’s bandwidth and on-chip tiling make possible. The roofline model, recapped for one machine in The roofline bound, tests whether the resulting data supply can feed the arithmetic units. Its ridge point, peak arithmetic divided by memory bandwidth, is the number of operations a kernel must perform per byte fetched before arithmetic rather than memory becomes the limit. This section uses the ridge point from here on and formalizes it for the H100 in section 2.3.2. Where each architecture places its bytes sets how much bandwidth those levers can draw on (figure 5), because moving the active working set closer to arithmetic increases available bandwidth while reducing the capacity that can reside there.

Figure 5 does not rank accelerators along a single performance axis; it maps the structural clusters where different architectures choose to place bytes. Edge devices in the lower band keep memory cheap and compact, but their bandwidth remains constrained.

The devices of Archetype C (Federated MobileNet) sit in that corner. On each device, the binding limit is the power envelope that bounds compute. A phone-class device sustains only 2 W to 3 W, while one H100’s thermal envelope is about 233× larger. A federated fleet therefore grows by adding devices rather than by moving up the memory tiers, and at fleet scale, as Three systems archetypes noted, communication and which devices participate in each round limit progress as well.

Within the data center tiers, HBM moves memory onto the accelerator package, buying enough capacity and enough bandwidth for active model state. Local-SRAM designs push the working set still closer to arithmetic, buying extraordinary bandwidth at the cost of much smaller resident capacity. That tier map explains why HBM is the next engineering response: it is not the fastest memory in the system, but it is the highest-bandwidth tier that can hold tens to hundreds of gigabytes next to the compute die.

↪ The power, thermal, and memory limits of phone-class edge devices are developed in Design Constraints.

2.3.1 HBM and the memory wall

An H100 can execute 1,979 TFLOP/s of low-precision matrix arithmetic, but the batch-1 weight-streaming example for our 175B model supplies too little reuse to approach that peak. In a bandwidth-floor model its arithmetic units would use well under 1 percent of the matrix peak while the weights stream in. The limiting resource is the speed of delivery relative to multiplication. Once weights fit through sharding or compression, the serving path must still deliver the active shards at the required rate. Additional arithmetic capacity alone does not raise a binding bandwidth ceiling. HBM increases that data supply within the package.3

3 HBM origin: Standardized by JEDEC in 2013 as a joint development between AMD and SK Hynix, originally for graphics cards. ML accelerators adopted HBM because neural networks exhibit the same bandwidth-hungry, capacity-moderate access pattern as high-end rendering. Each HBM generation has roughly doubled bandwidth (128 GB/s in HBM1 to 1.2 TB/s per stack in HBM3e), yet the gap between memory bandwidth and arithmetic throughput continues to widen, which makes HBM a necessary but never sufficient response to the memory wall.

4 SRAM energy (pJ/bit): Accessing a bit in SRAM (shared memory) consumes approximately 0.1–0.5 pJ, while fetching from HBM consumes 2–5 pJ. This 10\(\times\) energy difference is the physical driver behind kernel fusion: every intermediate result kept in SRAM instead of written to HBM saves both time and significant power and thermal headroom.

The memory hierarchy within a single accelerator spans orders of magnitude in both capacity and bandwidth. At the top sits the register file (256 KB per SM, about 33 MB across all 132 SMs), with hundreds of TB/s of aggregate bandwidth but minuscule capacity per thread. Below this lies the L1 cache and shared memory (SRAM),4 offering up to 228 KB per SM (about 29 MB total) with tens of terabytes per second of aggregate bandwidth. Further down sits the 50 MB L2 cache (~12 TB/s), and finally the 80 GB of HBM3 at 3.35 TB/s.

The bandwidth gap between registers and HBM is approximately 100\(\times\), but that ratio alone does not specify stalled time. Utilization depends on arithmetic intensity, the number of floating-point operations performed per byte moved from memory (FLOP/byte). An operand fetched from HBM to feed a single operation leaves the arithmetic unit waiting for most of each cycle; an operand reused across hundreds of operations amortizes its fetch. Tiling increases that reuse by breaking matrices into blocks that fit within shared memory and registers, then performing multiple multiply-accumulate operations before replacing the operands. Higher intensity in turn raises model FLOPs utilization (MFU), the useful model computation per second divided by the selected hardware peak. The three-tier staging pipeline in figure 6 shows where the reuse comes from. Matching slices of \(\GemmLeftOperand\) and \(\GemmRightOperand\) stream from HBM into on-chip shared memory and feed repeated matrix-multiply-accumulate (MMA) instructions while partial sums stay in registers, and the finished \(\GemmOutputMatrix\) tile returns to HBM only after accumulating across the entire inner dimension.

For our 175B model, each kernel must fit its live tiles and partial results within the resources available to its executing SMs. The aggregate 33 MB of register space is distributed across those SMs, not a single pool for the entire model’s working set. HBM supplies bulk capacity, while tile shape and scheduling determine how efficiently operands are reused near the arithmetic.

Definition 2.1: HBM

HBM is a 3D-stacked DRAM architecture used by ML accelerators, with through-silicon vias (TSVs) connecting memory dies vertically within each stack and a wide interface across the package interposer connecting the stack to the processor.

  1. Significance: HBM3 in the H100 delivers approximately 3.35 TB/s of memory bandwidth (roughly 17× DDR5’s ~200 GB/s) at roughly 2–5 pJ/bit vs. DDR5’s ~20 pJ/bit. This bandwidth sets the \(\Bandwidth\) term of the single-machine iron law given in Iron Law Mapping, and the H100’s FP16 ridge point is approximately \(989\,\text{TFLOP/s} / 3.35\,\text{TB/s} \approx 295\) FLOP/byte, so any operator with arithmetic intensity below 295 FLOP/byte (for example, single-request decode, which performs two FLOPs for every two-byte FP16 weight it reads) remains memory-bound even with HBM.
  2. Distinction: Unlike socketed DDR memory, which communicates across centimeters of printed circuit board (PCB) trace, an HBM stack sits beside the processor on the package. Its micrometer-scale vertical connections can be 1,000\(\times\) shorter than board traces, but the complete processor-to-memory path also crosses the interposer. The wide, short on-package interface raises bandwidth without relying on a proportionate increase in per-pin signaling rate.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that HBM solves the memory wall. HBM moves the wall rather than eliminates it: Tensor Core throughput \((\PeakRate)\) has scaled faster than HBM bandwidth across generations (from 900 GB/s with 125 TFLOP/s FP16 in V100 to 3.35 TB/s with 989 TFLOP/s FP16 in H100), so the ridge point continues rising and more operations remain memory-bound despite HBM improvements.

Traditional DDR memory connects to the processor through pins on the edge of a printed circuit board. Each DIMM communicates over a 64-bit bus, and even with multiple channels (8 channels is typical for a high-end server CPU), a modern server tops out at roughly 200 GB/s of aggregate memory bandwidth.

The physical distance from the DIMM slot to the processor die is measured in centimeters, and every centimeter of copper trace introduces capacitance, signal attenuation, and energy loss. At DDR5 data rates (4,800–6,400 MT/s per pin), the signal conditioning circuits must compensate for significant channel impairment, consuming substantial power per bit transferred. Increasing the data rate on these long traces requires progressively more power for signal conditioning, creating a diminishing-returns curve that DDR5 is already approaching.

HBM sidesteps that board-level degradation through 2.5D integration (figure 7). Instead of driving signals horizontally across centimeters of motherboard trace over a narrow 64-bit bus, it stacks DRAM dies vertically and places the stack beside the processor on a silicon interposer.

The vertical stacking changes how bandwidth scales. Rather than pushing signals faster through long copper traces (the DDR approach, which has diminishing returns), HBM multiplies the number of parallel signal paths. A single HBM stack bonds 8–12 DRAM dies, threading thousands of through-silicon vias (TSVs),5 copper pillars etched through each die, to form a 1024-bit-wide interface per stack, compared to 64 bits for a DDR5 channel. This 16\(\times\) wider interface is the main source of HBM’s bandwidth advantage; HBM need not exceed DDR’s per-pin signaling rate. The processor and HBM stacks share a package and communicate across the silicon interposer, shortening the electrical path relative to socketed DIMMs. This packaging primarily improves bandwidth and energy per transferred bit; it does not by itself imply that complete HBM load latency is only a few nanoseconds.

5 TSV (through-silicon via): A vertical copper pillar, 5–10 micrometers in diameter, etched through a silicon die to connect stacked layers. Originally developed for CMOS image sensors in smartphone cameras, TSVs enabled HBM by replacing centimeters of PCB trace with tens of micrometers of vertical silicon, roughly a 1,000\(\times\) shorter signal path, which is what lets a stack expose a 1024-bit interface within an accelerator’s power budget.

This shortened physical distance confers three direct electrical advantages. Transfer energy per bit drops by roughly 4–10\(\times\), falling from approximately 20 pJ/bit for DDR5 to roughly 2–5 pJ/bit for HBM because microscopic interconnects present substantially lower capacitance and require less driving current. Signal propagation delay also shrinks, although only by a fraction of a nanosecond, which is small next to the DRAM array and controller delays that dominate end-to-end access latency. In addition, reduced capacitive loading relaxes per-pin signaling demands, enabling the wide 1024-bit interface to deliver massive aggregate throughput without pushing clock rates into inefficient, high-power regimes. Together, these physical mechanisms explain why modern accelerators widen the memory interface across an interposer rather than driving off-package pins faster.

Checkpoint 2.1: HBM and the memory wall

These questions check whether the physical benefits of 3D-stacked memory are clear:

Set side by side in table 4, the bandwidth, interface-width, and energy differences all trace to placement and width rather than to faster signaling.

This bandwidth advantage comes at a price. HBM costs approximately $10–$15 per GB, compared to roughly $3 per GB for DDR5 server memory. For an H100 with 80 GB of HBM3, the memory alone represents approximately $800–$1,200 of the accelerator’s manufacturing cost. For a B200 with 180 GB of HBM3e, the memory cost rises to $1,800–$2,700 per accelerator, making HBM one of the most expensive components in the system.

Packaging adds manufacturing stages beyond fabricating the memory dies. The stacking process includes thinning, alignment, bonding, and vertical interconnection, so usable stack yield depends on both the dies and their assembly. Those yields compound: a 12-high HBM3e stack is usable only if every die and every bonding step succeeds, so even small per-step losses multiply into a complete-stack yield below that of a single DRAM die. The silicon interposer must also accommodate the processor and multiple HBM stacks. With each stack occupying roughly 100 mm², an illustrative large package that pairs a reticle-sized processor with several stacks needs an interposer exceeding 1,000 mm². Testing, repair provisions, and assembly yield all affect cost; die count alone does not determine how many complete stacks remain usable.

The supply chain dynamics of HBM production further affect its cost and availability. The HBM supply chain is highly concentrated among a small number of manufacturers, and the production processes (die thinning, TSV etching, die-to-die bonding) require specialized capital equipment that is fundamentally different from standard DRAM manufacturing. Expanding HBM production capacity requires 12–18 months of equipment procurement and qualification, so production cannot rapidly scale in response to demand surges. When demand outpaces supply, as it did following the explosion of interest in large language models in 2023, component lead times stretch across a similar horizon and prices can double.

For infrastructure planners, HBM availability can constrain accelerator delivery even when the memory specifications meet the workload’s needs. The procurement schedule must compare confirmed lead times for complete systems with those for networking, power, and cooling. The longest unresolved dependency sets the commissioning date; HBM is one candidate rather than the universal pacing component.

For our 175B model, the HBM alone in the 1,024-accelerator reference cluster represents roughly $0.8–1.2 million at 80 GB per accelerator (or $2.0–2.9 million at the MI300X’s 192 GB per accelerator) under the $10–$15/GB cost model introduced earlier. This cost-capacity trade-off explains why accelerators typically offer 80 GB to 192 GB of HBM while the host server provides 512 GB to 2 TB of DDR: the fast memory holds state touched every step (weight shards, activations, gradients, and usually the sharded optimizer state), while the cheap memory holds staging buffers, data loading queues, and colder state that capacity pressure forces out, such as offloaded optimizer shards.

The boundary between what resides in HBM and what resides in DDR is a critical design parameter for training frameworks, and managing this boundary efficiently is one of the key challenges addressed by ZeRO (Zero Redundancy Optimizer) sharding and offloading strategies. Sharding divides optimizer, gradient, and parameter state across workers; offloading places colder state in host DRAM or Non-Volatile Memory Express (NVMe) when HBM capacity is the binding limit. Getting this boundary wrong in either direction is costly: placing too much data in HBM wastes expensive capacity, while placing too much in DDR creates bandwidth stalls that idle the arithmetic units.

↪ Memory-efficient sharding of optimizer, gradient, and parameter state is developed in Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP.

Lighthouse 2.1: Archetype B (DLRM at Scale): The capacity wall
Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) has so far exposed the bandwidth side of the memory wall. Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) exposes the capacity side, the boundary between fast and cheap memory just examined. An embedding table of 10 TB or more cannot fit into the 80 GB HBM of a single H100, nor can it fit into the aggregate HBM of a single 8-GPU node (640 GB nameplate, about 687 GB in decimal bytes). Capacity alone requires at least 15 such nodes before accounting for replication, hot-table headroom, and bandwidth balance. Sharding makes lookup routing and result exchange part of the request path. An AllToAll exchange sends distinct portions to different participants; its traffic depends on the batch and placement. Clearing the capacity limit therefore creates a second question about bandwidth and latency.

HBM generations and the scaling boundary

HBM has grown alongside model sizes, but far more slowly than parameter counts, which is why capacity pressure keeps pushing models across devices. Each generation increases the number of stacked dies, the signaling rate per pin, and the total capacity per stack, driven by the relentless growth in model parameters. Table 5 shows the scaling boundary: HBM4 must widen the interface because pin-rate increases alone are no longer enough.

As table 5 shows, the transition from HBM3 to HBM3e is particularly significant for our running example. Using decimal bytes consistently, an A100 with 85.9 GB of HBM2e can hold 24.5 percent of our 175B model’s weights (at FP16). An H100 with 85.9 GB of HBM3 can hold the same fraction but deliver the data 64 percent faster. A B200 with 193.3 GB of HBM3e can hold 55.2 percent of the weights and deliver them at about 8 TB/s. None of them can hold the full model, which is why multiple accelerators are needed in a node (section 2.5).

Quantization changes the capacity story for inference. Storing weights in INT8 or INT4 halves or quarters the footprint, and section 2.8.1 works through how many accelerators the running model then needs. The capacity constraints that drive the need for multi-accelerator nodes for training (where FP16 or BF16 precision is typically required) are substantially relaxed for inference (where INT8 or INT4 quantization is often acceptable). This is another reason why training and inference infrastructure have different optimal configurations.

The projected jump to HBM4 doubles the interface width from 1024 bits to 2048 bits for the first time since HBM’s introduction. This signals that per-pin signaling rate increases alone cannot sustain bandwidth growth indefinitely, and the bus must widen. The doubling of interface width requires a correspondingly larger interposer area for routing, which is one reason accelerator packages have tended to grow as bandwidth targets rise.

The projected 12–16 TB/s HBM4-class package range shows the limit of bandwidth gains alone, using the same weight-streaming floor as the running model’s preview at the start of this section. A dense 1T-parameter model requires roughly 2 TB of FP16 weight storage. Streaming those weights through one B200-class path at about 8 TB/s gives 2000 GB/8 TB/s = 250 ms for one batch-1 weight load. At the assumed 16 TB/s, that floor becomes roughly 125 ms. Neither path has been shown to hold the full model, and even the faster floor limits a single path to about eight tokens per second for one request. A responsive trillion-parameter service must therefore split its weights across many memory paths, shrink them with reduced precision, or both; which combination depends on the latency target and the quality the reduced precision preserves.

An important architectural consideration is the number of HBM stacks per accelerator and how they connect to the processor. The H100 has 5 HBM3 stacks, each providing approximately 670 GB/s, for a total of 3.35 TB/s aggregate bandwidth. A B200-class Blackwell GPU package uses 8 HBM3e stacks for an aggregate of about 8 TB/s. The number of stacks is constrained by the interposer area available for HBM placement: each HBM stack occupies approximately 100 mm² of interposer area, and the total interposer must accommodate both the processor die(s) and all HBM stacks. Larger interposers allow more HBM stacks (and therefore higher bandwidth and capacity) but are more expensive and harder to manufacture.

The interposer area constraint creates a concrete design tension. Making the processor die larger (more Tensor Cores, more SMs) leaves less interposer area for HBM stacks, potentially reducing bandwidth. Making the processor die smaller (fewer Tensor Cores) frees interposer area for more HBM but reduces peak compute throughput. The optimal balance depends on the target workload’s position relative to the ridge point. Compute-bound workloads, whose arithmetic intensity sits above it, benefit from a larger processor die (more Tensor Cores), while bandwidth-bound workloads below it benefit from more HBM stacks, so a workload’s arithmetic intensity predicts which side of the balance it falls on before any silicon is committed.

Memory bandwidth and token latency

The distinction between memory capacity (how many gigabytes the HBM can store) and memory bandwidth (how many terabytes per second it can deliver) separates two infrastructure questions. Capacity determines whether a model’s weights fit, with headroom for the remaining live state. Bandwidth limits how fast the model can run when data movement sets the critical path. Dense autoregressive decode at batch size 1 supplies a useful example because each weight serves only one token before the next weight load. The next three calculations step down from the running model to Llama-3 70B. Its FP16 weights span two H100s rather than the running model’s five, and the H200 comparison that follows needs a model whose serving behavior a real memory upgrade can change; the arithmetic carries over to the 175B model by scaling the bytes.

Napkin Math 2.1: The physics of token latency
Problem: A weight-only model of batch-1 decode for Llama-3 70B uses 141.2 GB of FP16 weights. To isolate the two resource demands, assume hypothetical capacity sufficient to hold them while retaining one H100-equivalent compute rate and HBM path. Which term gives the larger time estimate: arithmetic or weight transfer?

  1. Compute time: Each token requires \(2 \times 70 \times 10^9 = 1.4 \times 10^{11}\) floating-point operations. At 989 TFLOP/s of FP16/BF16 Tensor Core peak throughput, the arithmetic takes \(\IronLawComputeTime \approx \text{\textbf{0.14}}\text{ ms}\).
  2. Memory time: Loading 141.2 GB of weights from HBM at 3.35 TB/s takes \(\IronLawMemoryTime \approx \text{\textbf{42.1}}\text{ ms}\).

Systems insight: The memory term accounts for 99.7 percent of the sum of these two idealized times. That ratio identifies a bandwidth-dominated estimate; it is not a measurement of cycles spent idle. Raising compute throughput alone barely changes this estimate. A faster memory path reduces the weight-transfer floor, while actual token latency also includes the other memory traffic, communication, and execution costs omitted here.

Holding that weight load fixed and changing only the memory system shows how directly each HBM generation moves the floor. For the same 70.6B FP16 model, with 141 GB of weights, the single-path streaming floor is roughly 141 GB/2.04 TB/s = 69.2 ms at A100-class bandwidth, 141 GB/3.35 TB/s = 42.1 ms at H100-class bandwidth (the notebook’s memory term), and 141 GB/8 TB/s = 17.7 ms at B200-class bandwidth, falling nearly in proportion to each bandwidth gain. As in the notebook, each case isolates one accelerator-equivalent HBM path even though the full FP16 model does not fit on one A100 or H100. These bounds screen memory bandwidth against a latency target; whether token latency improves by the same ratio depends on the other work on the request’s critical path. The cleanest test of that dependence compares two accelerators that differ only in their memory system.

Napkin Math 2.2: The memory wall: H100 vs. H200
Comparing the NVIDIA H100 against the H200 isolates a memory-system upgrade for large language models (LLMs). While both chips share the same compute cores (same peak TFLOP/s), the H200 provides 1.4× higher memory bandwidth and 1.6× more HBM capacity.

This comparison uses a Llama 3 model with 70.6B parameters, sequence length 2,048, and batch size 1. Both cases retain FP16 weights and the hypothetical capacity convention of notebook 2.1: each streams the complete payload through one accelerator-equivalent HBM path, even though it cannot fit on one H100. The comparison isolates path latency rather than establishing two feasible single-device deployments.

The reported speedup comes from a serving-latency model whose inter-token latency adds two terms to the weight-streaming time, the key-value cache reads for the request’s context and a fixed per-layer kernel-launch overhead, so it is not a raw bandwidth ratio alone. The weight-only estimate provides a comparison. Its memory term accounts for 99.7 percent of the summed compute and transfer times, so reducing that term suggests a gain near the HBM bandwidth ratio, 4.8 TB/s \(\div\) 3.35 TB/s \(\approx\) 1.4×.

Systems insight: For the modeled configuration, the latency model predicts an H200 speedup of 1.4× over the H100 despite equal peak arithmetic throughput. Its agreement with the bandwidth ratio supports the memory-bound diagnosis for this case. Changing batch size, context length, or partitioning requires evaluating the new configuration; the estimate does not establish one bottleneck for all autoregressive models.

The modeled memory-time share, 99.7 percent in this example, indicates the memory-boundedness of this workload under the stated assumptions. A workload this memory-bound gains almost nothing from more TFLOP/s and nearly proportionally from more TB/s, which is what the H200 comparison shows, so bandwidth per dollar rather than peak arithmetic is the useful procurement metric for this regime. A measured execution timeline is still needed to determine how much transfer overlaps computation and which delays remain exposed, which keeps a hardware-screening calculation separate from a performance forecast.

The generational trend of figure 4 widens this regime with every refresh. Because peak arithmetic outgrew HBM bandwidth from V100 to B200 (18× against 8.9×), the arithmetic intensity required to reach the compute ceiling rose about 2×, so workloads without commensurate reuse remain bound by data movement on each newer device.

For training workloads, reusing weights across more tokens can shift the constraint toward arithmetic throughput. The relevant reuse is the number of tokens that share each weight read on one accelerator, so a large global training batch does not by itself establish high local arithmetic intensity; it must arrive at each device as a large local token count. The Roofline Model tests the compute and bandwidth ceilings for the actual operation and batch shape before either specification becomes a procurement metric.

2.3.2 Roofline model

The token latency calculation in notebook 2.1 makes memory bandwidth the relevant procurement metric for the batch-1 workload. The roofline model of Williams, Waterman, and Patterson (Williams et al. 2009) tests whether that diagnosis survives a change in batch shape, precision, or accelerator by relating the workload’s arithmetic intensity to the candidate accelerator’s compute and bandwidth ceilings.

Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/1498765.1498785.

For an H100 (989 TFLOP/s FP16, 3.35 TB/s HBM), the ridge point that this section has relied on since its opening is about 295 FLOP/byte; most LLM inference operators fall well below this threshold, which is why the roofline remains the first diagnostic tool for identifying whether more compute or more bandwidth will improve performance. The quantity that places a workload on the plot is the arithmetic intensity introduced with tiling above, written \(\ArithmeticIntensity\) in FLOP/byte.

↪ Placing measured kernels on the roofline by arithmetic intensity is developed in The roofline model.

Decode sits deep in the memory-bound regime, far below the ridge.

The roofline model expresses the maximum achievable performance of a workload as the lesser of two ceilings:

\[ \text{Achievable FLOP/s} = \min\left(\PeakRate,\ \Bandwidth \times \ArithmeticIntensity\right) \tag{1}\]

Equation 1 defines a performance ceiling. At low arithmetic intensity, memory bandwidth supplies the lower of the two ceilings, which grows linearly with \(\ArithmeticIntensity\) along the sloped line. At high intensity, peak arithmetic supplies the lower ceiling, which no longer rises with \(\ArithmeticIntensity\). A kernel’s measured rate can remain below either roof because the model does not include every dependency, scheduling limit, or execution overhead.

Definition 2.2: Ridge point

Ridge point is the ML accelerator arithmetic intensity where the memory bandwidth ceiling meets the compute ceiling \((\RooflineRidgePoint = \frac{\PeakRate}{\Bandwidth})\).

  1. Significance: It defines the Hardware Efficiency Threshold. Below the ridge point, bandwidth supplies the lower modeled ceiling \((\Bandwidth)\); above it, arithmetic supplies the lower ceiling \((\PeakRate)\).
  2. Distinction: Unlike Peak FLOP/s (which only describes the horizontal ceiling), the ridge point describes the Balance of the architecture. A rising ridge point over hardware generations indicates that compute is growing faster than bandwidth, making utilization harder.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that all GPUs have the same ridge point. In reality, it varies by Precision: because \(\PeakRate\) is higher for INT8 than FP32 while \(\Bandwidth\) is constant, the ridge point for INT8 is much higher, requiring more data reuse to saturate the hardware.

Machine learning workloads land in different regions of the roofline plot depending on the operation and the batch configuration, not on the model family. Single-request autoregressive decode streams the full weight tensor (two bytes per parameter in FP16) from HBM to perform two floating-point operations per parameter, an arithmetic intensity of about 1 FLOP/byte, far below the H100’s ridge point of 295 FLOP/byte. The token-latency calculation in notebook 2.1 showed that the arithmetic finishes in microseconds while the weight movement takes milliseconds. Every other regime in table 6 comes from reusing each byte fetched from HBM across more operations. Decode also reads the key-value (KV) cache, the stored attention keys and values of earlier tokens, and because each request has its own cache, batching does not amortize that traffic.

The table’s numbers are idealized reuse counts, so they mark directions rather than measurements. Channel depth, tensor layout, fusion, precision, and cache residency determine where a convolutional layer lands. Attention also resists a fixed label. Full-sequence attention can reuse keys and values across many queries, whereas single-token decode reads the cache for one query per head. The resulting placement depends on shape and implementation. The analytical objective is to classify each execution phase and batch configuration rather than assign a permanent regime to a model family.

A measured workload point below its roofline ceiling needs further diagnosis. Dependencies, insufficient parallel work, irregular accesses, synchronization, and power limits can all prevent a kernel from approaching that ceiling. The gap identifies a question for profiling; it does not establish that software optimization can recover the entire difference.

The Roofline’s diagnostic power extends beyond individual kernels to entire training runs. For our 175B model, the computation graph contains thousands of distinct operations with different arithmetic intensities. The dense feed-forward network layers are dominated by large general matrix multiplications (GEMMs) with high arithmetic intensity, placing them firmly in the compute-bound regime where Tensor Core utilization is the bottleneck. Conversely, operations like layer normalization and element-wise activations possess low arithmetic intensity, sitting deep in the memory-bound region where the compute units idle while waiting for data. Attention kernels can land on either side of the ridge, depending on the shapes and reuse examined above, and long decode contexts add key-value traffic without comparable reuse. This diagnostic distinction dictates the optimization strategy: memory-bound layers benefit from kernel fusion (reducing HBM round-trips), while compute-bound layers benefit from precision reduction (moving from FP16 to FP8, which effectively raises the hardware’s compute ceiling).

Whole phases spread even wider than individual operators. The idealized intensities of decode and training differ by 2,048×, which is why the same hardware shows very different utilization across them. Batch-1 decode sits near 1 FLOP/byte, far left of the ridge and deeply bound by memory bandwidth, while large-batch training near 2,048 FLOP/byte crosses the ridge and reaches the compute roof (figure 8). The training point assumes that 2048 tokens on one accelerator share each weight read, the local reuse that a large global batch alone does not guarantee, so it counts tokens rather than sequences and local rather than global batch. It is a contrast case, not a universal intensity for training. Actual placement requires counting bytes at the memory level being modeled and accounting for tensor shapes, fusion, and reuse.

The table’s batching rows can be turned into a utilization bound. For inference serving, batching multiple requests together moves the workload point rightward on the plot, because each weight read is multiplied against more activations. Under the weight-traffic-only bound, batch-1 decode on an H100 can use at most 0.34 percent of the FP16 peak. At batch size 64, each weight read from HBM serves every request in the batch, the intensity rises to 64 FLOP/byte, and the bound climbs to about 22 percent of peak, a 64× gain that still leaves the workload below the ridge. The linear growth holds only in this approximation; key-value cache traffic, which batching does not amortize, activation traffic, and capacity limits lower both figures in practice. The gain has a cost. Waiting to assemble a batch can introduce queuing latency, although a serving policy can instead dispatch a smaller batch when a latency deadline approaches. Serving systems must carefully balance this trade-off between throughput (larger batches, higher utilization) and latency (smaller batches, faster response per request).

For training, batch size affects both statistical convergence and hardware efficiency. A larger local batch can increase reuse and parallel work, but only while capacity and other bottlenecks permit. Changing the global batch can also require learning-rate and schedule adjustments to preserve training quality. The relevant comparison is time and resources to reach the target quality, rather than utilization in isolation.

For fleet procurement, the roofline is a screening test rather than a throughput forecast. It rules out upgrades that raise a nonbinding ceiling, while the gap below the relevant ceiling identifies work still needed in software or communication. A candidate that fits the local workload must also fit the node’s memory budget, the interconnect, and the facility power envelope.

The Roofline Model also helps distinguish two kinds of kernel optimization. If a workload is 10\(\times\) below the compute ceiling but already touching the bandwidth ceiling, increasing arithmetic throughput alone does not improve that bound. Reducing memory traffic or increasing reuse can move the bound through batching, kernel fusion, or quantization. These changes still require checking numerical quality, capacity, and latency before treating the predicted gain as usable performance.

Collected into a single procurement comparison for the running model, the two ends of the landscape give opposite hardware answers.

Example 2.2: Roofline analysis: Training vs. inference
Scenario: An engineering team evaluates hardware choices for serving (decode batch size 1) vs. training (batch size 2048) a 175B parameter model on an H100 GPU (ridge point 295.2 FLOP/byte).

Diagnosis: The weight-traffic model gives single-token decode arithmetic intensity \(\ArithmeticIntensity=\) 1 FLOP/byte \(\ll\) 295.2 FLOP/byte, bounding throughput to 0.34 percent of the selected peak FLOP/s. The assumed training reuse raises intensity to \(\ArithmeticIntensity=\) 2,048 FLOP/byte \(\gg\) 295.2 FLOP/byte, making arithmetic the lower roofline ceiling. This placement identifies the relevant bound without establishing achieved throughput.

Systems lesson: Hardware selection starts with the phase’s arithmetic intensity and memory footprint. The modeled training phase benefits from arithmetic capacity, while batch-1 decode benefits from bandwidth. Sustained throughput at the required quality and latency determines whether either advantage justifies its cost.

That same diagnostic becomes most valuable when a proposed upgrade changes the wrong ceiling.

Checkpoint 2.2: Roofline diagnosis

A team is serving a 13B-parameter model at batch size 1 on an H100 and observing 25 ms per token. The profile shows low tensor-core utilization during decode.

Memory capacity and bandwidth screen out infeasible accelerator choices. For the candidates that remain, fleet sizing also needs a sustained compute rate at a precision the workload can use.

Self-Check: Question

  1. HBM3 provides a much wider interface and lower signaling energy per bit than socketed DDR5. Which packaging mechanism explains these advantages?

    1. HBM3 runs individual pins at \(16\times\) higher clock frequency than DDR5, overcoming PCB trace attenuation through high-frequency signaling.
    2. HBM3 uses a novel DRAM cell structure that eliminates capacitor refresh cycles, reducing dynamic power consumption.
    3. HBM3 stacks DRAM dies vertically with through-silicon vias (TSVs) and connects the stack to the processor through a wide, short silicon-interposer path on the package.
    4. HBM3 compresses weight matrices on-die, transferring fewer physical bits across the memory bus.

  2. An engineering team compares an H100 with a hypothetical successor offering twice the dense FP16 peak but the same HBM bandwidth. Hold the FP16 weight payload, partition, and batch-1 shape fixed, assume sufficient capacity, and consider a weight-streaming model whose bandwidth ceiling is lower than its compute ceiling on both devices. What changes in the modeled token-latency floor, and which levers could lower it?

  3. A custom attention kernel has arithmetic intensity of 1.2 FLOP/byte on an H100 under the FP16 roofline convention. Which change most directly raises its memory-bandwidth performance ceiling, subject to measuring the resulting execution?

    1. Use SRAM-sized attention tiles and fusion to avoid materializing the full score matrix in HBM and reduce intermediate HBM round-trips.
    2. Overclocking Tensor Cores to increase peak FP16 TFLOP/s, raising the horizontal ceiling of the roofline.
    3. Adding dummy memory reads to artificially raise the measured memory traffic of the kernel.
    4. Offloading the layer normalization and attention operations to host CPU DRAM over PCIe Gen5.

  4. True or False: Thermal Design Power (TDP) represents the maximum instantaneous power a GPU can draw during a single clock cycle, so cooling infrastructure should be sized for the average power draw observed across an entire training step.

  5. Arrange the multi-tier memory staging hierarchy in an NVIDIA H100 accelerator in order of operand flow during a tiled GEMM execution, from initial off-chip storage to final arithmetic execution: (1) Tensor Cores, (2) Streaming Multiprocessor (SM) Register File, (3) On-package HBM3, (4) Shared Memory (on-chip SRAM).

  6. In the Roofline Model, the specific arithmetic intensity where the diagonal memory bandwidth slope meets the horizontal peak compute ceiling (\(I = R_{\text{peak}} / \text{BW}\)) is called the ____.

See Answers →

2.4 Sustained Compute and Precision

A fleet sized from advertised matrix throughput can miss its training deadline when operations cannot use the advertised precision or keep the arithmetic units busy. The relevant hardware contract includes both the eligible matrix operations and their sustained contribution to the complete workload. That contract begins with the specialized arithmetic units: Tensor Cores6 on NVIDIA GPUs and Matrix Multiply Units (MXUs) on Google TPUs.

6 Tensor Core: Introduced with NVIDIA Volta (2017) as \(4{\times}4{\times}4\) FP16 fused matrix-multiply-accumulate units. Later generations widened format support: Turing (2018) added INT8, Ampere (2020) added TF32/BF16, and Hopper (2022) added FP8. A lower-precision mode can raise the arithmetic ceiling, as in Hopper’s \(2\times\) FP8-versus-FP16 peak, but the gain depends on the specific format and hardware. It is not a rule that every added format doubles throughput.
