Power-envelope implications recur throughout the infrastructure hierarchy. At the node level, configured limits constrain how many accelerators can share a chassis (section 2.5). At the rack level, aggregate sustained load and heat flux help determine whether engineered air or liquid cooling can meet the design point (section 2.6.2). At the pod level, coincident demand and transient behavior shape megawatt-scale power delivery (section 2.7). Every level above the die must therefore manage both sustained heat and electrical variation. Accelerator selection must compare useful workload throughput within that facility envelope, rather than treating power as a cost added after the compute decision.

2.6.2 Rack design considerations

The physical design of an ML rack differs substantially from a traditional server rack in ways that go beyond power and cooling. In a conventional 42U rack, servers are installed as independent 1U or 2U pizza-box units, each with its own fans, power supplies, and cable connections. ML racks use a fundamentally different form factor. A DGX H100 occupies 8U and contains 8 GPUs, the NVSwitch fabric, multiple power supplies, and (in liquid-cooled configurations) coolant manifolds with quick-connect fittings. Four DGX units in a 42U rack leave only 10U for networking switches, cable management, and PDUs.

The transition from traditional server form factors to ML-optimized designs is driven by the same power density challenge that motivates engineered airflow or liquid cooling. A traditional 1U server dissipates 300–500 W and requires only modest airflow for cooling. A DGX H100 node dissipates approximately 8.4 kW across its 8 GPUs, NVSwitches, and supporting components, requiring either massive airflow (in air-cooled configurations) or liquid cooling plumbing (in liquid-cooled configurations). The form factor must accommodate both the compute hardware and the thermal management infrastructure, which is why ML nodes are substantially taller (8U vs. 1U) than traditional servers.

Cable management is a nontrivial engineering challenge. Each DGX H100 node has eight InfiniBand cables (one per GPU), two Ethernet management cables, power cables, and, for liquid-cooled units, coolant hoses. A fully populated rack contains over 40 high-speed cables whose layout is governed by three strict physical constraints: airflow clearance, minimum bend radius, and electromagnetic interference. In air-cooled designs, cables must not obstruct intake or exhaust airflow, because even a localized blockage creates hot spots that trigger thermal throttling. High-speed cables simultaneously enforce a minimum bend radius (typically 10–15\(\times\) the outer diameter for active optical cables), below which optical fibers suffer attenuation and induce bit errors or complete link drops. Densely bundling high-speed copper cables can also induce electromagnetic crosstalk that degrades signal quality. In large installations, the cable plant alone can take weeks to install and test, and cable routing errors are one of the most common causes of post-installation debugging delays.

As rack power densities climb toward 100 kW for liquid-cooled clusters, power conversion and chassis volume become important design budgets. Traditional designs dedicate volume in every chassis to redundant AC power supply units (PSUs), while many dense-rack designs use rack-level DC power distribution. In this architecture, a centralized power shelf converts mains AC to a 48V DC busbar that runs the height of the rack, replacing individual server AC supplies. Power reaches the server backplane through the 48V bus, and local DC-to-DC converters supply the lower voltages required by the components. Consolidation can recover chassis volume for cooling loops and high-bandwidth interconnects, but it does not by itself establish fewer conversion stages or a fixed efficiency gain. The comparison must account for each design’s actual stages and conversion efficiency across the intended load range. Shared power shelves and busbars also create a rack-level failure boundary whose redundancy and serviceability must be qualified.

The rack’s network attachment needs the same density-driven rethinking, starting with the top-of-rack (ToR) switch. In traditional data centers, a ToR switch provides 1–10 Gb/s Ethernet connectivity to each server, aggregated into uplinks to higher-level switches. In an ML rack, the ToR switch is an InfiniBand or high-speed Ethernet switch providing 400 Gb/s per port, and it must handle the bursty, synchronized traffic patterns of distributed training.

The placement and configuration of ToR switches affects which exchanges remain local. In a conventional design, all 8 GPUs in a node connect to the same ToR switch, so when every GPU sends its share of a data-parallel AllReduce at once, the traffic of all eight converges on one switch and its uplinks. A rail-optimized design instead assigns each GPU position to its own “rail”: GPU 0 in every node connects to leaf switch 0, GPU 1 to leaf switch 1, and so on. Data-parallel collectives typically run among GPUs with the same index on different nodes, so a rail-aligned group’s traffic stays within a single switch when all its endpoints attach there. Larger rail groups can span switches and still use the fabric above them; the benefit depends on how the job’s process groups map onto those paths.

The rail-optimized design spends additional switches, ports, and cabling to align communication paths with accelerator rails. Its benefit depends on the collective traffic matrix, routing, oversubscription (a switch tier with less uplink than downlink capacity, so traffic leaving it competes for fewer links), and job placement rather than a fixed efficiency gain.

↪ How rail-optimized topologies align cabling with collective traffic is developed in Rail-optimized topology.

Power delivery networks and transient dynamics

A training step makes each accelerator’s current draw jump. When a collective barrier releases a node’s GPUs from a communication phase into dense arithmetic, each one steps from a few hundred watts toward its full power envelope within microseconds, and the barrier correlates those steps across the node. The power delivery network (PDN) must hold the supply voltage steady through that step, and the difficulty grows as the voltage falls from the 48V busbar to the sub-1V rail at the die.

At a fixed power, current scales inversely with voltage, so the core rail carries far more current than the busbar; the voltage regulators transform voltage and current between those boundaries. What disturbs the supply is the rate of change of that current, its slew rate \(d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent/d\TimeInstant\). Every conductor loop has inductance, a property that resists changes in current, so a fast current step produces a momentary voltage dip, or droop, even when average power remains within the facility budget.

Definition 2.7: Power delivery network (PDN) and transient dynamics

Power delivery network (PDN) is the electrical distribution hierarchy that supplies the processor through regulators, interconnects, and decoupling capacitors. Parasitic loop inductance contributes to voltage fluctuations during changes in current.

  1. Significance: Synchronized workload transitions can correlate accelerator demand. Supply excursions outside the device’s specified limits can cause timing errors, protective throttling, or resets.
  2. Distinction: Local electrical transients require a faster response than facility power-control loops can provide. Regulators and nearby charge storage address different timescales within the same hierarchy.
  3. Common pitfall: Applying the full core-current slew to a single inductance summed from rack to die. Regulation and decoupling change the current waveform at each stage; the relevant inductance and current must belong to the same path.

For a particular inductive path, the voltage excursion contributed by its inductance follows equation 3:

\[\FiniteChange \SupplyVoltage= \ParasiticLoopInductance\frac{d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} \tag{3}\]

Here \(\ParasiticLoopInductance\) is the loop inductance of that path, and \(d\AcceleratorSupplyCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) is the current slew through it. Local capacitors initially supply part of a load-current increase, so the current slew seen upstream need not equal the slew at the core. A complete rail model also accounts for resistance, capacitance, and regulator response.

A worked estimate for one accelerator shows why the hierarchy matters. When an H100 moves from its communication-phase draw of about 400 W to its 700 W envelope on a 0.8 V core rail, its core current rises by about 375 A. Suppose the board loop between the voltage regulator and the package has 50 pH of inductance. If package capacitors supply the fast edge so that this loop sees the step over 1 μs, equation 3 gives a droop of about 19 mV, or 2.3 percent of the rail. If the same step reached that loop in 100 ns, the droop would be about 190 mV, roughly 23 percent of the rail and far outside a 5 percent operating band. The inputs are scenario assumptions, but the tenfold sensitivity to rise time is physical. The same current step stays inside tolerance or corrupts timing depending on which charge store answers it first.

The collective barrier that aligns transitions from communication to arithmetic raises the correlation of demand across a rack, but it does not imply that every accelerator switches on the same nanosecond or that their core currents flow through one shared low-voltage loop. Engineers must evaluate both each device’s local voltage excursion and the aggregate demand seen by upstream supplies.

A rising load can produce voltage droop; an abrupt load reduction can produce overshoot. Droop below the specified operating minimum can invalidate timing margins. Overshoot must remain within the device’s specified limits as well. Neither the magnitude nor the resulting failure mode follows from workload synchronization alone.

The mitigation is a hierarchy of charge storage and control, in which each tier answers the transients too fast for the tier above it (table 12). Placement and parasitic impedance determine which frequencies each tier can address.

Workload-level controls complement these hardware tiers. Power ramping can reduce abrupt demand changes where the hardware and firmware support it, but a design must verify those controls rather than assume that every kernel launch receives automatic smoothing. The fleet-level lesson is that average power capacity and transient voltage integrity require separate checks.

2.6.3 Facility reliability and environmental controls

Rack design also defines physical failure domains. Data centers housing ML infrastructure implement multiple layers of physical security and environmental monitoring both for regulatory compliance and because a single rack of DGX H100 nodes ($1.4 million in hardware alone) concentrates enough value, heat, and liquid-cooling risk to justify controls that would be excessive for traditional server equipment.

Environmental monitoring is therefore part of the compute design rather than a facilities afterthought. Water detection sensors beneath raised floor tiles and around liquid cooling piping can identify leaks within seconds and trigger automatic coolant isolation valves. Aspirating smoke detection systems continuously sample air from inside racks, detecting combustion byproducts at concentrations far below what sprinkler-activating detectors can sense. Vibration sensors on the building structure and rack frames catch seismic events or construction-induced vibration that could damage disk drives or loosen cable connections. Humidity control keeps relative humidity between 40–60 percent to prevent both static discharge and condensation.

These environmental controls are not afterthoughts but integral parts of the infrastructure design that affect reliability and uptime. A water leak that reaches a DGX H100 baseboard can cause millions of dollars in damage and weeks of downtime. A fire in a single rack can shut down an entire data center hall for days while the fire suppression system is recharged and the affected area is decontaminated. The 2021 OVHcloud fire in Strasbourg shows how a single-building incident, absent the right compartmentalization, escalates into a site-wide failure domain.

War Story 2.1: The data center that became the failure domain (2021)
Context: OVHcloud operated four data centers at its Strasbourg site, serving customers whose systems depended on the site’s physical power, fire, and recovery design (OVHcloud 2021).

Mechanism: On March 10, 2021, a fire broke out in building SBG2. Uncontained fire spread and shared power pathways destroyed building SBG2, damaged adjacent SBG1, and forced emergency shutdown of SBG3 and SBG4.

Impact: Thousands of servers were physically destroyed, taking over 3.6 million websites offline worldwide and destroying unbacked-up customer data.

Fix: OVHcloud rebuilt the site with independent physical fire barriers, isolated power distribution per building, and mandatory free off-site backup storage.

Systems lesson: Compute infrastructure extends beyond GPUs and network topology. Fire compartments, power isolation, backup geography, and disaster-recovery assumptions determine whether a rack-scale incident becomes a site-scale outage. Multi-node ML training clusters inherit this physical dependency: a single data-center power or cooling domain failure halts active training runs and destroys un-replicated checkpoint states unless recovery paths cross physical facility boundaries.

OVHcloud. 2021. Strasbourg Datacentre: Latest Information. OVHcloud incident update.

Once rack-level controls are in place, the reliability question moves up to facility redundancy. The same failure-domain logic that isolates a leaking rack also decides how much duplicate power, cooling, and recovery capacity the site should buy.

Data center reliability is commonly classified using the Uptime Institute Tier system, with Tier III and Tier IV representing common options for mission-critical compute (Uptime Institute 2026). A Tier III facility is concurrently maintainable: redundant capacity components and distribution paths let operators maintain or replace equipment without shutting down IT operations. Tier IV adds fault tolerance through independent, physically isolated systems, so a single equipment failure or distribution-path interruption should not affect IT operations. The practical difference is not a magic availability number; it is how much failure prevention the building buys in electrical and mechanical infrastructure before software-level resilience takes over.

Uptime Institute. 2026. Tier Classification System. Uptime Institute web page.

For ML training, the calculus differs from traditional enterprise computing. A training run is a long-running batch process, not a real-time transaction system, so a facility outage during a two-week run usually costs a restart from the last checkpoint rather than permanent data loss, provided a valid checkpoint survives in an accessible failure domain and usable compute becomes available. The restart still incurs lost work, recovery time, and potentially a missed deadline. Many training deployments therefore pair concurrently maintainable (Tier III) facilities with aggressive application-level fault tolerance: rather than buying every layer of building-level redundancy, they invest in checkpointing, job restart, and storage that can absorb rare facility interruptions, and redirect the avoided resiliency spend toward accelerators, networking, or storage. Facility redundancy and application-level recovery address different parts of the same risk. A design can trade some preventive infrastructure for checkpointing and recovery capacity only after checking which failures the recovery path survives and whether the resulting completion-time risk is acceptable.

Checkpoint 2.3: Infrastructure physics

A team is planning to deploy 256 H100 GPUs (32 nodes, each drawing about 8.4 kW as computed in the rack design section) in an existing air-cooled data center with 250 kW of available facility power, a PUE of 1.5, and a cooling plant that can reject 200 kW of IT heat.

The rack concentrates power and heat into a physical volume where thermodynamics, not software, sets the limits. A single rack of 32 GPUs, however, is far from sufficient for our 175B model, which needs tens to hundreds of nodes, many racks’ worth, to train on a practical schedule. The next level of infrastructure aggregates tens to hundreds of racks into a unified computing system: the pod, whose physical interconnect fabric must be engineered around the communication patterns and failure domains the workload creates.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A four-node DGX H100 rack draws approximately \(33\text{ kW}\) of IT power. Which heat-transfer property can make direct-to-chip liquid cooling attractive as rack density rises, even though engineered air cooling remains possible in a suitable facility?

    1. Liquid cooling increases GPU arithmetic intensity by lowering electrical resistance inside Tensor Cores.
    2. Water has much higher volumetric heat capacity than air, so it can carry away the same heat with a much smaller volumetric flow for a comparable temperature rise.
    3. Air cooling causes optical transceivers to melt when exposed to fan airflow velocities above 5 meters per second.
    4. High-density racks require full dielectric immersion because cold plates cannot conduct heat from silicon heat spreaders.

  2. A facilities team provisions its cooling and electrical infrastructure for the average GPU power observed during training (\(\approx 500\text{ W}\) per GPU) rather than the \(700\text{ W}\) sustained design target. Explain why this choice can silently degrade cluster training throughput even when no hardware errors or crashes occur.

  3. Synchronous ML training can correlate accelerator power ramps across a cluster. How does this electrical behavior distinguish its power-delivery requirements from those of largely uncorrelated web-serving workloads?

    1. ML clusters operate on direct current exclusively, eliminating the need for uninterruptible power supplies.
    2. Web-serving data centers experience higher \(di/dt\) transients because individual HTTP requests draw more peak current than matrix multiplications.
    3. ML training consumes purely reactive power that causes step-down transformers to freeze mechanically.
    4. Correlated demand changes require checking both local regulator and decoupling response and the aggregate demand seen by upstream supplies, rather than sizing solely for average power.

  4. Facility A reports a Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) of 1.08 on an electrical grid with carbon intensity of \(450\text{ g CO}_2/\text{kWh}\). Facility B reports PUE 1.35 on a grid with \(50\text{ g CO}_2/\text{kWh}\). For a \(10\text{ MW}\) IT compute load, calculate the total hourly electrical power drawn and carbon emissions for both facilities, and explain the limitation of using PUE as a sole environmental metric.

  5. True or False: Replacing individual server AC supplies with a centralized 48V DC power shelf and busbar guarantees one fewer conversion stage and a 2–3% electrical efficiency gain.

See Answers →

2.7 The Pod

Training our 175B model on a single DGX H100 node (8 GPUs, 15,832 TFLOP/s of aggregate FP8 peak) would take about 1.4 years at the efficiency assumed below, even if the model state could fit. Roughly 37 nodes bring the compute-only time down to two weeks, and about 512 nodes bring it to a single day, before any communication or recovery overhead. A pod is the cluster-scale unit that makes this possible: many racks under one high-speed fabric, power envelope, and cooling design. Pod design is the first point where topology, power, and failure domains become shared infrastructure rather than per-rack concerns. Figure 12 contrasts two dominant pod-scale fabrics: a two-tier leaf-and-spine fat tree providing full bisection bandwidth across all node uplinks, and a 2D torus wiring each node to its immediate neighbors with \(\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ClusterNodeCount})\) bisection scaling to reduce switch count and exploit traffic locality.

The engineering challenge of the pod is wiring those nodes together into a network fast enough to keep gradient synchronization from becoming the bottleneck. The pod aggregates tens to hundreds of racks into a single, coordinated computing system where the network fabric serves the same role that the system bus serves within a single machine.

The scale of this challenge is worth appreciating concretely, and the pod-scale examples that follow deliberately step up to eight times the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. A 1,024-node DGX H100 cluster contains 8,192 GPUs, 4,096 NVSwitch chips, 8,192 InfiniBand HCAs, several hundred InfiniBand switches, tens of thousands of cables, and draws approximately 8.6 MW at the modeled rack IT boundary. It occupies 256 four-node racks across one or more data center halls.

The physical weight of such a cluster is also substantial. Each DGX H100 node weighs approximately 130 kg (288 lb), so 1,024 nodes weigh over 133 metric tons. Combined with racks, switches, cables, and cooling infrastructure, the total weight can exceed 300 metric tons. The data center floor must be structurally engineered to support this concentrated load, with typical floor loading requirements of 1,500–2,500 kg per square meter for ML clusters, compared to 500–1,000 kg per square meter for traditional server installations. This structural requirement is often overlooked during facility planning and can be a blocking constraint for retrofitting existing buildings.

The physical layout of the data center hall reflects these density constraints. The extreme power density of ML training racks necessitates rigid hot aisle/cold aisle containment in air-cooled sections, where cold air is forced into the enclosed front of the rack and waste heat is captured immediately at the rear exhaust. The “spine” of the hall, the central cable corridor connecting all racks to the aggregation switches, must accommodate thousands of fiber optic cables and power feeds. Overhead cable trays are preferred over under-floor routing to improve airflow and accessibility, carrying the heavy copper power feeds and fragile fiber interconnects that form the nervous system of the cluster. The layout must also accommodate the liquid cooling infrastructure: coolant distribution unit placement, coolant piping runs, and isolation valves that allow individual racks to be serviced without draining the entire cooling loop. These physical layout decisions, made during facility design, constrain the network topology options available to the training team years later.

Training a 175B model on this cluster requires about six floating-point operations per parameter per training token, two in the forward pass and four in the backward pass, or \(6 \times 175 \times 10^{9} \times 300 \times 10^{9}\) in total (assuming 300 billion training tokens). The token count is where the scaling laws of AI Scaling Laws enter the hardware plan, because a compute-optimal allocation would give a model of this size many times more tokens than GPT-3 saw, multiplying this budget, and the pod-hours it consumes, by the same factor. Assuming local compute efficiency of 45 percent before communication and pipeline losses, the aggregate compute rate is \(8,192 \times 1979 \times 10^{12} \times 0.45\) FLOP/s, or about 7.30 EFLOP/s, yielding a compute-only baseline of approximately 12 hours. On the 1,024-GPU reference cluster itself, the same budget at the same efficiency would take about 4 days. This assumed local efficiency is not measured distributed MFU, because MFU over a distributed step already includes its communication and pipeline losses. In the terms of the Fleet Law (equation 1.3), this baseline is the \(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount\) term alone.

In practice, communication overhead, pipeline bubbles, checkpoint I/O, hardware failures, and maintenance windows compound to extend the actual wall-clock time beyond that baseline. The first two are the Fleet Law’s exposed \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) and \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\) terms, and the rest are operational losses around the step. Notebook 2.4 chains these factors into a scenario estimate of wall-clock time; production schedules can stretch further once queueing, data readiness, debugging, reruns, and site-specific operational margins enter the plan. The central theme is unchanged: at pod scale, the schedule depends on both local execution and fleet overhead. Recovering this lost time is the central challenge of distributed systems engineering, and the solutions span hardware (better networks), software (overlapped communication), and operations (proactive maintenance to minimize downtime).

Napkin Math 2.4: Training time for 175B
The training time for our 175B model derives from first principles.

  1. Total FLOPs: Using the same \(6 \times \Parameters\times \DatasetSize\) approximation, where \(\Parameters = 175 \times 10^9\) and \(\DatasetSize = 300 \times 10^9\) tokens: \(3.15 \times 10^{23}\) FLOPs

  2. Cluster throughput: 8,192 H100 GPUs at 1,979 TFLOP/s peak, assuming 45 percent local compute efficiency before distributed overhead: \(8,192 \times 1979 \times 10^{12} \times 0.45\) FLOP/s before distributed overhead \(\approx\) 7.30 EFLOP/s

  3. Compute-only training baseline: (\(3.15 \times 10^{23}\) FLOPs)/(\(8,192 \times 1979 \times 10^{12} \times 0.45\) FLOP/s) \(\div 3600 \approx\) 12.0 hours

  4. Illustrative overhead chain: The scaling factor already includes pipeline fill and drain, so no separate bubble step follows. Each subsequent percentage adds time relative to the preceding subtotal, not to final wall time; the factors are scenario assumptions, not independent measured losses:

    • Distributed-step losses (scaling efficiency 0.56, the modeled tier for 8,192 GPUs, covering communication and pipeline bubbles): \(\div\) 0.56 \(\rightarrow\) 21.4 hours
    • Checkpoint overhead (\(\approx 3\%\)): \(\times 1.03 \rightarrow\) 22.1 hours
    • Hardware failures and restarts (\(\approx 10\%\) of the checkpoint-adjusted subtotal): \(\times 1.10 \rightarrow\) 24.3 hours
    • Maintenance windows (\(\approx 5\%\)): \(\times 1.05 \rightarrow\) 25.5 hours

The total training time is 25.5 hours in this simplified scenario chain, or about 1.1 days. Real production calendars may still stretch to weeks after queueing, debugging, reruns, and site-specific operational margins are included. Recovering this lost time is the domain of distributed systems engineering.

2.7.1 The pod as a warehouse-scale computer

At this scale, the warehouse-scale computer (WSC) described in The Datacenter as a Computer (Barroso et al. 2019) requires a matched budget for arithmetic and communication. Rounding the pod up to 10,000 H100 GPUs, about ten times the reference cluster, keeps the warehouse-scale arithmetic easy to follow, and the chapter’s other fleet-scale power, cost, and failure examples use the same round figure. Such a WSC supplies approximately 9.89 EFLOP/s of FP16 peak and 19.8 EFLOP/s at FP8. Non-blocking InfiniBand at 50 GB/s per GPU, the injection bandwidth each endpoint can push into the fabric, provides 500 TB/s of aggregate injection bandwidth and 250 TB/s across the bisection, the cut that splits the cluster into two equal halves. With 4:1 oversubscription at that boundary, meaning the switch tier above it has one quarter as much uplink as downlink capacity, the modeled crossing capacity falls to 62.5 TB/s. The effect on a collective depends on how much traffic crosses the constrained paths, its schedule, and competing transfers. The bandwidth ratio alone is not a collective-time multiplier.

Barroso, Luiz André, Urs Hölzle, and Parthasarathy Ranganathan. 2019. The Datacenter as a Computer: Designing Warehouse-Scale Machines. Synthesis Lectures on Computer Architecture. Springer International Publishing. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-01761-2.

↩︎ The warehouse-scale computer as the unit of fleet design is established in The shift to the warehouse-scale computer.

Treating the building as one computer, rather than as a room full of computers, changes how engineers approach its physical design. In a traditional data center, each server is an independent unit with its own operating system, storage, and network identity. The facility simply provides power, cooling, and physical security. Servers can be added, removed, or replaced independently, and the workloads running on different servers are unrelated to each other.

In a WSC, a server assigned to a distributed training job contributes to a shared completion time. Its standalone throughput is useful only if the other participants, network, and storage can sustain that rate. For the running model, a node must obtain additional memory capacity or offload state, and a practical training schedule also requires more compute. The network fabric, distributed storage, and fleet orchestrator make those resources available as one training system.

Coupling distinguishes a WSC from a general-purpose cluster. A synchronous training job couples the workers in its own process groups, so a failed worker can stall that job while other tenants continue, unless a shared power, cooling, or network failure reaches them as well. Designing such a machine is building-level computer architecture. Rack placement, the power delivery chain, and the network topology must be co-designed, because a mistake at any layer propagates through the others.

The first physical consequence concerns communication. The network can set the scaling ceiling: within a single node NVLink supplies enough bandwidth for tensor parallelism, but across nodes the fabric must carry gradient tensors, activation checkpoints, and pipeline stage outputs, so a building-scale machine is only as fast as the wires that bind its cores. The WSC reframing makes the interconnect a first-class architectural component rather than a peripheral.

Mean time between GPU failures falls inversely with GPU count.

The second consequence is that failure is a physical certainty, not an exceptional event. The arithmetic is the one behind figure 1. Under the scenario assumption of independent failures and a per-GPU MTTF of approximately 50,000 hours, a cluster of 10,000 GPUs has a mean interval of roughly five hours between GPU failures, and the 25K-GPU fleet of that figure about two. That is an expectation, not a periodic schedule, and shared power or cooling faults add correlated exposure. The node failure estimate of section 2.5.5 and the facility controls of section 2.6.3 are the same exposure at smaller scope. Redundant infrastructure, such as dual-path power feeds and redundant switch fabrics, can prevent some interruptions; checkpoint storage and recovery capacity limit lost progress when a running job must stop. Both belong in the physical design. Redundancy keeps many component failures from halting the job, and checkpoints bound the work lost when one does.

The third consequence is that the building blocks combine differently depending on the workload they must carry. A pod is not assembled from a single canonical part list; the interconnect, the switching, and the memory placement are each chosen against the communication and capacity pattern the dominant workload imposes. Table 13 contrasts three pod designs. An NVIDIA SuperPOD wires GPUs through an InfiniBand fat-tree for any-to-any flexibility, a Google TPU pod hard-wires chips into a torus for dataflow throughput, and a recommendation pod built for Archetype B arranges the same class of accelerators around embedding memory rather than raw compute.

The recommendation pod is the clearest case of memory placement, not compute, driving the layout, and it is the physical form of Archetype B (DLRM at Scale). Its embedding tables reach 10 TB or more, which lighthouse 2.1 showed would need at least 15 DGX H100 nodes of HBM merely to hold, while the dense neural-network layers that operate on those embeddings are comparatively small. A recommendation-first design splits the two across the memory hierarchy. The terabyte-scale embedding tables reside in host CPU DRAM (roughly $3–$5/GB), and the dense layers execute on GPUs, where the compute density justifies the $10–$15/GB cost of HBM. Embedding tables create a capacity requirement, but small random lookups can also saturate aggregate memory bandwidth or expose access latency at high concurrency. Host placement trades capacity cost against lookup throughput and host-to-accelerator transfer, so the workload must validate both budgets. The systems lesson is that optimal infrastructure is workload-specific, not specification-maximizing. Deploying compute-optimized nodes for a memory-capacity-bound workload would strand most of the accelerator’s throughput on a problem its silicon was never the bottleneck for.

The physical design supplies constraints, not an operating policy; later chapters turn its power, capacity, topology, reliability, and sustained-throughput measurements into placement, recovery, monitoring, and ownership decisions. One hardware decision remains for this chapter. With every level from the accelerator to the warehouse-scale computer in view, the choice of accelerator can now be judged against the whole hierarchy rather than against its data sheet.

↪ The ownership decision, in which sustained fleet utilization sets the build-versus-buy threshold, is developed in Utilization as the economic invariant.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Barroso and Hölzle’s Warehouse-Scale Computer (WSC) thesis reframes an ML pod from ‘a room full of computers’ to ‘a single computer that fills a room.’ Which practical consequence most directly shapes infrastructure engineering at pod scale?

    1. The entire facility is engineered as a unified machine where the network fabric acts as the system bus, distributed storage acts as the disk subsystem, and the cluster orchestrator acts as the operating system.
    2. All servers share a single hardware cache-coherence bus across the entire building, eliminating the need for message-passing software.
    3. Workloads are structured as embarrassingly parallel, uncoordinated batch jobs that completely isolate nodes from one another.
    4. Component failure rates decrease at warehouse scale, allowing fault tolerance to be managed as an offline manual process.

  2. A team doubles a distributed training cluster from 1,024 to 2,048 GPUs and observes wall-clock training time drop from 14 days to 10 days rather than the ideal 7 days. Calculate the realized scaling efficiency, determine whether the cost per useful FLOP increased, and identify the next diagnostic step.

  3. A synchronous training job spans 10,000 GPUs. Assume a hard crash is detected and recovered from a valid checkpoint, while a persistent gray failure remains undetected and delays dependent steps. Why can the gray failure cost more useful work over a long run?

    1. Gray failures permanently corrupt the model weights, forcing the entire training run to restart from step zero.
    2. Gray failures trip circuit breakers in the rack, causing cascading facility blackouts.
    3. The hard crash incurs detection, restart, and lost-work costs, while the undetected straggler can repeatedly delay the participating job until it is diagnosed.
    4. Gray failures disable FP8 Tensor Cores, silently falling back to double-precision FP64 and exhausting memory.

  4. The fully provisioned switched topology illustrated in the pod section uses leaf, spine, and, at larger scale, core tiers to supply full bisection bandwidth. It is called the ____ topology.

  5. Arrange the stages in the worked scenario’s accounting sequence: begin with the compute-only baseline, add the distributed-step losses, then checkpointing, restart, and maintenance allowances. (1) Checkpoint I/O overhead (\(\approx 3\%\)), (2) Compute-only training time (\(6PD\) FLOPs divided by aggregate peak throughput times assumed local compute efficiency), (3) Hardware failure and restart overhead (\(\approx 10\%\)), (4) Scaling efficiency covering communication and pipeline bubbles (\(\approx 0.56\)), (5) Maintenance overhead (\(\approx 5\%\)). Each additive percentage is relative to the preceding subtotal.

See Answers →

2.8 Accelerator Selection for Fleet Workloads

An accelerator’s compute throughput, memory bandwidth, memory capacity, and power consumption are meaningful only in relation to a specific workload. The same H100 that sustains 30–50 percent of peak during well-tuned large-batch training may achieve less than 5 percent utilization during single-request inference, because the binding constraint shifts from arithmetic to memory bandwidth. Selecting the right accelerator therefore requires mapping each workload archetype to its position on the Roofline plot and identifying which physical resource dominates its performance.

For the 175B running example, large local matrix operations can place substantial training work above the Roofline ridge point. Arithmetic throughput then matters alongside the capacity needed for sharded state and the interconnect needed by the partition. H100, B200, and TPU v5p configurations are candidates to evaluate under the same workload and quality target. The selected software stack must express the required operations efficiently enough for the hardware advantage to appear in sustained execution.

Serving the same language model changes the answer, for the reason the token-latency notebook 2.1 established. At batch size 1, the H100’s 3.35 TB/s bandwidth determines decode throughput while much of the arithmetic silicon waits, so the key metric becomes memory bandwidth per dollar rather than peak TFLOP/s. Batching helps for the reason section 2.3.2 quantified, reusing each weight read across more requests, which is why serving systems work so hard to batch without violating tail-latency service-level objectives (SLOs). The workload did not change names, but the binding term of the single-machine iron law moved from arithmetic to data movement, and the accelerator choice changes with it.

Recommendation models split the decision across sparse lookup and dense computation. Each request retrieves many small vectors from terabyte-scale tables with poor locality, so the tables create a capacity requirement while lookup concurrency determines access latency and bandwidth demand. The dense neural network layers have a different reuse pattern. A hybrid CPU-GPU design can place tables in host DRAM and dense computation on accelerators, but its transfer and lookup costs must be compared with accelerator-resident sharding or caching. One headline TFLOP/s number cannot describe the complete request path.

Vision and diffusion workloads move back toward arithmetic throughput because convolutions, attention blocks, and denoising networks reuse data heavily. Diffusion inference makes this especially clear. A standard Stable Diffusion v1.5 configuration runs 50 denoising steps, so the service pays for a sequence of full model evaluations rather than one decode step per token. For these workloads, TFLOP/s per dollar often dominates bandwidth per dollar once model state fits in memory.

Mixture of experts (MoE) adds a routing constraint: the sparsely gated layer activates only part of a much larger parameter set for each token (Shazeer et al. 2017). Mixtral-8x7B (Jiang et al. 2024), for example, has 46.7B total parameters but only 12.9B active parameters per token because the router chooses 2 experts from 8 experts. That compute saving introduces a many-to-many token exchange pattern, the AllToAll collective, and load-balance risk because tokens must be routed to different expert placements. High-bisection fabrics and load-balancing mechanisms such as auxiliary losses, which penalize overloaded experts, or capacity factors, which cap how many tokens each expert accepts, determine whether the theoretical saving appears in the training trace (Fedus et al. 2022; Lepikhin et al. 2021).

Shazeer, Noam, Azalia Mirhoseini, Krzysztof Maziarz, Andy Davis, Quoc Le, Geoffrey Hinton, and Jeff Dean. 2017. “Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Jiang, A. Q., A. Sablayrolles, A. Roux, A. Mensch, B. Savary, C. Bamford, D. S. Chaplot, et al. 2024. “Mixtral of Experts.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2401.04088.
Fedus, William, Barret Zoph, and Noam Shazeer. 2022. “Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 23 (120): 1–39.
Lepikhin, Dmitry, HyoukJoong Lee, Yuanzhong Xu, Dehao Chen, Orhan Firat, Yanping Huang, Maxim Krikun, Noam Shazeer, and Zhifeng Chen. 2021. “GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

↪ MoE AllToAll routing and expert load imbalance are developed in Dynamic routing and expert imbalance in MoE AllToAll.

Multi-modal models combine phases with different arithmetic intensity and batching behavior, such as vision encoding and text decode. Their relative cost and placement determine whether flexible scheduling, additional memory, or communication capacity changes the critical path. Modality count alone does not select an accelerator architecture; the planner must follow the data exchanged between the actual phases.

Systems Perspective 2.3: Matching hardware to workload
Workload diversity can justify a heterogeneous fleet, with each pool provisioned for its binding resource. The gain comes from matching hardware to the work assigned to it, while keeping each tightly synchronized training group balanced. The fleet-wide decision must account for that matching benefit together with the cost of supporting multiple platforms.

2.8.1 Precision and throughput trade-offs

The formats and peak rates of section 2.4 become fleet decisions once they are priced. The FP8 doubling has a capacity-equivalent dollar value, and quantization changes how many devices serving needs.

FP8 doubles the H100’s advertised arithmetic ceiling for supported operations, from 989 TFLOP/s to 1,979 TFLOP/s per GPU; at fleet scale that doubling has a price tag. For a 10,000-GPU cluster priced at roughly $350,000 per 8-GPU node, the increase in advertised arithmetic capacity is equivalent to adding another 10,000 GPUs, or roughly $438 million in node hardware. This is a peak-capacity comparison; it is not a prediction of training speedup or a saving that can be booked before workload validation.

The E4M3 and E5M2 trade-offs described with the formats are why the doubling is not free. Scaling places a tensor within the format’s range but does not restore the precision lost to rounding in only 3 mantissa bits. Values outside the representable range can saturate or become nonfinite, and sufficiently small values can round to zero. The selected recipe therefore needs workload-specific quality validation even when Transformer Engine supplies the scaling machinery. Infrastructure sizing must use the sustained rate of a validated precision policy.

For inference, quantization changes both weight storage and the possible hardware topology. Our 175B model at FP16 requires 350 GB, necessitating a minimum of five 80 GB H100 GPUs for the parameters alone. At INT8, the weight payload drops to 175 GB, fitting on three GPUs. At INT4, it shrinks to 87.5 GB, fitting on two GPUs. These are weight-only capacity bounds; quantization metadata, attention state, and workspace also need memory. Reading 4-bit rather than 16-bit weights quarters the raw payload, but it does not change physical bandwidth or guarantee a fourfold token-latency reduction. Kernel support, conversion work, other traffic, and any change in device count determine the realized gain, subject to validation of model quality.

2.8.2 Energy cost of data movement

The Roofline Model and token latency analysis demonstrate that data movement limits performance. Data movement also dominates energy consumption, with direct implications for fleet economics. A useful normalization is one FP16 operand. Reading one 16-bit value from HBM at roughly 4 pJ/bit costs about 64 picojoules (pJ), while one FP16 multiply-accumulate operation costs roughly 1 pJ. The raw operand fetch can therefore cost about 64\(\times\) the arithmetic operation, before tiling and reuse amortize that movement across many MACs.

Data movement dwarfs arithmetic in energy cost.

This operand-level ratio explains why accelerator architects devote so much silicon area to data reuse. A Tensor Core’s tile-based execution model loads a small matrix into local registers and reuses each element hundreds of times (once per element in the opposing matrix), amortizing the HBM access cost across hundreds of 1 pJ compute operations. Without this reuse, the energy budget would be dominated by memory access, and most of the chip’s power would be spent heating wires rather than switching transistors.

At fleet scale, movement energy must be evaluated over the execution schedule. A 10,000-GPU cluster operating within a 700 W per-GPU envelope also powers control, interconnect, and other circuitry; the operand-level ratio does not determine the share of that envelope spent on memory. Kernel fusion and FlashAttention reduce particular transfers, while activation checkpointing trades saved activation storage for recomputation. The useful comparison is total energy to complete equivalent work at the required quality, including any change in execution time.

The energy perspective provides a physical foundation for understanding why different parallelism strategies have different efficiencies. Tensor parallelism requires data movement over NVLink (approximately 5 pJ per bit at the link level, plus the energy of the NVSwitch chips). Data parallelism requires data movement over InfiniBand (approximately 15–20 pJ per bit, including the energy of the HCA and switch). Pipeline parallelism moves less data but requires more complex scheduling. Each parallelism strategy represents a different point in the trade-off between communication energy and computation efficiency.

Napkin Math 2.5: The energy cost of data movement
At modern process nodes, moving data costs far more energy than computing on it. The energy hierarchy of a single operation at the 4 nm node makes the gap concrete.

Take the operand-level ratio above one tier further. One FP16 multiply-accumulate (MAC), the atomic unit of deep learning, costs about 1 pJ, and fetching its 16-bit operand from HBM costs about 64 pJ. Fetching the same operand from off-package DRAM at roughly 20 pJ per bit costs about 320 pJ, 320\(\times\) the MAC itself, so standard DRAM multiplies the HBM penalty another fivefold.

The macro impact appears when serving our 175B model. Generating a single token requires loading the entire 350 GB weight tensor from HBM:

\[\text{Data Movement Energy} = 350 \text{ GB} \times 8 \text{ bits/byte} \times 4 \text{ pJ/bit} \approx \mathbf{11.2 \text{ Joules}}\]

The computational cost for the associated \(\approx\) 175 billion MACs (about 350 billion FLOPs) is trivial by comparison:

\[\text{Computation Energy} = 175 \times 10^9 \text{ MACs} \times 1 \text{ pJ/MAC} \approx \mathbf{0.175 \text{ Joules}}\]

The whole-model ratio lands on the same 64\(\times\) as the single-operand comparison above, because at batch size 1 each weight fetched feeds exactly one multiply-accumulate; only reuse separates the two. This energy penalty is why HBM is engineered for physical proximity to the GPU die; shorter traces mean less capacitance and lower energy per bit. It also explains why techniques that reduce data movement, such as quantization (moving fewer bits) and kernel fusion (preventing round-trips to memory), are the primary levers for improving both system performance and power efficiency.

2.8.3 Benchmarking accelerator performance

A procurement team comparing accelerators needs a published number that predicts the performance the organization will actually achieve. Peak TFLOP/s, memory bandwidth, and TDP describe potential, not achieved performance, so the decision turns on whose measured results to trust and under what rules they were produced. The industry-standard reference is MLPerf, maintained by MLCommons: MLPerf Training reports the time to train reference models to a target accuracy, and MLPerf Inference reports serving throughput and latency under realistic batching and streaming conditions. Both capture complete system performance, including software stack efficiency, communication overhead, and memory management, rather than raw silicon speed alone.

Closed-division MLPerf results constrain the workload and quality target to support comparisons across implementations. They still permit specified optimizations and benchmark-dependent tuning; the rules do not require identical hyperparameters or ordinary untuned framework configurations in every case. Open-division submissions allow broader changes and require closer inspection of what differs. Neither division predicts a deployment automatically. Compare the submission’s workload, software, precision, hardware count, and quality target with the intended system before using its result for procurement.

For our 175B model, training, fine-tuning, and serving need separate measurements. Batch shape, trainable state, sequence length, and partitioning can change both capacity demand and the critical path. Fine-tuning does not occupy a fixed point between training and decode merely because it updates an existing model. Evaluate the intended phases on candidate hardware with the actual model and data pipeline, using published MLPerf results as comparative evidence rather than deployment forecasts.

Systems Perspective 2.4: Beyond peak specifications
When evaluating accelerator options, the following metrics provide a more complete picture than peak TFLOP/s alone:

  • MFU: The ratio of achieved FLOP/s during real training to peak hardware FLOP/s. An MFU of 50 percent means the model’s useful computation averages half the chosen peak rate over the measurement interval; it does not distinguish partially occupied execution from completely idle time.
  • Time-to-train: The wall-clock time to train a reference model to a target metric. This captures all system-level effects that a steady-state MFU measurement can omit, including I/O stalls, checkpointing overhead, and job restart delays outside its measurement window. Whole-run MFU includes those elapsed-time losses but still does not establish the model quality reached.
  • Cost per token: For language model training, the total cost (hardware amortization plus electricity) divided by the number of tokens processed. This metric normalizes across different hardware generations, cluster sizes, and pricing models.
  • Tokens per dollar: The inverse of cost per token. Divide tokens per second by dollars per second, converting an hourly rental price to the same time unit before comparing systems.

No single metric tells the whole story. A system with high MFU but high cost per GPU-hour may be less economical than a system with lower MFU but cheaper hardware. The right metric depends on whether the organization is optimizing for time (training must finish by a deadline), cost (minimize total expenditure), or throughput (maximize tokens processed per unit time).

2.8.4 Inference-specific infrastructure considerations

Serving trained models changes the planning objective. A large training run is one long job that occupies its allocation for days or weeks and is judged by sustained throughput and time to a target quality. Its steps repeat the same exchanges, so communication demand is easier to characterize than an arrival stream, and an individual operation can take milliseconds to seconds without harm, although failures, I/O, and changes in execution shape still affect the schedule. Serving must instead turn the same hardware into responses for many independent, short-lived requests arriving at irregular intervals, each under a latency target that for interactive use is measured in tens of milliseconds.

Serving infrastructure must meet latency targets at an acceptable cost per query under a variable request load. Independent requests can be assigned to separate replicas, but a replica that shards a model across devices still communicates within each request. Prompt processing, successive decode steps, and complete responses also have different latency budgets. Capacity planning must therefore specify the request mix, model placement, and service targets before concluding that serving needs less network capacity than training.

The differing requirements drive distinct hardware and architecture choices, summarized in table 14.

The table shows why serving cannot be planned as training with smaller batches: the optimization target changes from synchronized throughput to latency-bounded, continuously billed requests.

The implication is that effective serving infrastructure often uses different hardware, different software, and different facility designs than the training infrastructure. Some organizations use previous-generation GPUs (A100s) for serving because the memory bandwidth per dollar can be competitive with newer GPUs, and the lower TDP (400 W vs. 700 W) allows higher rack density and lower cooling costs.

The inference workload itself bifurcates into two distinct phases with opposing bottlenecks. The prefill phase processes prompt tokens in parallel, increasing weight reuse and potentially approaching the compute ceiling when the prompt and batch shapes supply enough work. The decode phase generates tokens sequentially for each request and is deeply memory-bandwidth-bound at small batch sizes. To maximize hardware utilization, high-performance serving systems employ continuous batching, which schedules requests at the iteration level rather than the request level. Completed requests leave the active batch and waiting requests can enter at iteration boundaries. Scheduling prefill alongside decode also requires controlling how much prompt work delays the next decode iteration.

A napkin estimate shows how quickly serving capacity reaches training scale. One tensor-parallel replica of the running model on an eight-GPU node streams its FP16 weights once per decode step, which takes at least 13.1 ms at H100 HBM bandwidth. Batching 32 concurrent requests yields about 2,450 tokens/s per replica. A service handling 1,000 requests/s at 250 output tokens each must generate 250,000 tokens/s, which requires at least 103 replicas, or 824 GPUs, most of the 1,024-GPU training reference cluster. The estimate is a lower bound because it ignores key-value cache traffic, prefill work, and headroom for bursts and failures; Batching Strategies at Scale replaces it with batching models that account for key-value cache capacity, variable request lengths, and latency targets.

Reliability objectives differ as well. A training job can absorb an occasional failure because checkpointing recovers the lost progress, whereas a stalled serving replica delays every request routed to it until traffic fails over.

2.8.5 The accelerator decision matrix

A practical decision framework starts with the workload’s likely limiting resources and tests them against its execution shape. Table 15 supplies candidate configurations under representative assumptions, not fixed assignments of model families to bottlenecks or vendors. The selection rule is to diagnose the binding constraint, then compare sustained useful work within the capacity, quality, latency, and facility budgets. The table’s constraint column is the per-device one, what a single accelerator must supply. The introduction’s archetype roster answers a different question, which constraint binds the fleet as a whole, so a workload can be compute-bound on each device and still communication-bound across the cluster, as Archetype A is.

For our 175B running example, table 15 separates the training purchase from the serving purchase. The workload constraint comes first; software support, deployment lifetime, and measured sustained throughput are compared only among candidates that clear it. A cheaper accelerator-hour is useful only if it supplies the required throughput within the capacity, latency, and facility budgets.

Checkpoint 2.4: Accelerator selection

An engineering team needs to deploy a 70B-parameter model for both training and inference. Training will use batch size 2048 across 256 GPUs for 3 months. Inference will serve 10,000 requests per second at batch size 1 for 2 years.

Each of these selections assumes the walls stay where they are. New technologies do not remove the walls; they move them. The technologies in section 2.9 matter because they change where the memory, communication, or power constraint binds.

2.9 Infrastructure Technologies That Move the Walls

A new data center will host three or four generations of accelerators over its 15-year structural lifetime, so future-facing technologies matter only when they change a durable planning constraint. The useful test is not whether a technology is novel, but which wall it moves, what new wall appears after the move, and which fleet-design decision changes as a result. Compute Express Link (CXL) moves the memory-capacity boundary. Wafer-scale integration moves the chip-to-chip communication boundary. Advanced packaging moves the die boundary inside the accelerator package. Optical circuit switching moves the fabric topology and power boundary. None of them repeals the physics of data movement; each relocates the place where infrastructure teams must spend power, cooling, cost, and software complexity.

2.9.1 Compute express link (CXL)

CXL builds on the PCIe physical layer with protocols for I/O, caching, and memory access. Supported device types and platform configurations determine which of those capabilities are available. Memory expanders and pooled memory can make host capacity less rigid, but accelerator access also requires a supported data path and software placement. Fleet planning must establish those capabilities before treating a pool as available training memory.

For ML training, the natural target is cold or phase-local state rather than the active matrix-multiply working set. The Adam optimizer state for our 175B model occupies 1,400 GB, and it is touched during parameter updates rather than streamed through every Tensor Core operation. A shared CXL pool could therefore reduce per-node memory pressure by holding optimizer shards, embedding-table spillover, or local data-cache buffers outside the accelerator HBM tier. The training framework still has to schedule this state carefully, but the placement problem becomes less binary than “fits in this node” or “spills to storage.”

The new wall is bandwidth. A representative CXL memory path over PCIe Gen5 x16 provides 64 GB/s, while H100 HBM provides 3.35 TB/s. That 52× gap means weights and activations for the forward and backward passes still belong in HBM. CXL is a capacity tier, not an HBM replacement. The fleet-design implication is concrete: planners should ask whether server platforms, rack layouts, and orchestration software can absorb CXL memory pooling, not whether CXL removes the need for high-bandwidth accelerator memory.

HBM remains 52× faster, so CXL is capacity only.

2.9.2 Wafer-scale planning constraint

At the opposite extreme from CXL sits the wafer-scale engine introduced in section 2.2.1. Its on-wafer fabric removes package- and board-level transfers for mapped operations, but it does not make communication free. Infrastructure planning instead shifts toward capacity, placement, and injection bandwidth.

Our 175B running model needs 350 GB for FP16 weights alone, far beyond the 44 GB of on-wafer SRAM. Cerebras addresses this through weight streaming: external MemoryX units feed weights to the wafer over a 1.2 TB/s path while the wafer executes each layer, decoupling model capacity from the on-wafer memory. Weight streaming was designed for training. Activations stay on the wafer while weights stream in layer by layer and gradients stream back to MemoryX, which performs the optimizer update. The approach works when each layer’s computation is large enough to hide the streaming time. The wafer trades the communication wall for a streaming-bandwidth wall.

The lesson for infrastructure planners is not that wafer-scale systems replace GPU fleets. It is that memory proximity and memory capacity trade against each other. WSE-style designs buy extraordinary local bandwidth by putting the working set near the arithmetic, while CXL buys flexible capacity by moving memory farther away. HBM sits between those extremes: off-chip but on-package, smaller than host memory but fast enough for the active computation. The right architecture is the one whose memory tier matches the workload’s reuse pattern.

2.9.3 Advanced packaging

Underpinning both directions is the evolution of advanced packaging. Packaging moves the boundary inside the accelerator package rather than all the way out to the rack or all the way in to a single wafer, and it matters because the reticle limit is only about 858 mm² for a single exposure. 2.5D interposers, such as TSMC’s CoWoS, mount compute dies and HBM stacks on a shared silicon substrate, allowing chiplet-based accelerators to behave like a single device for most software. 3D stacking, with approaches such as SoIC, places SRAM caches or logic directly above one another and shortens data paths further. The H100-to-B200 transition shows the fleet consequence: the package can deliver far more local arithmetic and die-to-die bandwidth, but the rack must absorb higher power density, tighter cooling requirements, and less tolerance for power transients before the silicon advantage becomes useful infrastructure.

2.9.4 Reconfigurable topologies and optical circuit switching

While CXL and advanced packaging address memory and die boundaries, warehouse-scale computing increasingly encounters an interconnect power and topology constraint. In modern clusters spanning tens of thousands of accelerators, conventional multi-tier Clos networks, the switched leaf-and-spine family of figure 12 whose full-bisection form is the fat tree, connect groups of endpoints through successive tiers of electrical packet switches. These switches inspect each packet and choose an outgoing link; the alternative considered here changes the physical connections between groups for a longer-lived workload allocation. Every network hop incurs an Optical-Electrical-Optical (OEO) conversion: incoming photons hit a photodiode, high-speed SerDes circuits deserialize the electrical signal, switch ASICs buffer and route packets across crossbars, and output SerDes drive lasers to re-transmit light over optical fiber.

At 800 Gb/s and 1.6 Tb/s per-port data rates, SerDes and transceiver electronics consume significant energy, and every additional switch tier repeats that conversion cost for each bit that crosses it. Fixed electrical Clos topologies are also cabled statically, locking the facility into a single oversubscription ratio and rigid bisection pattern regardless of whether the running workload is pipeline-dominated, data-parallel, or all-to-all expert-routed.

Definition 2.8: Optical circuit switching

Optical circuit switching (OCS) is a physical networking architecture that routes optical signals directly using Micro-Electro-Mechanical Systems (MEMS) mirror arrays without Optical-Electrical-Optical (OEO) conversion, enabling dynamic, software-defined topology reconfiguration and high energy efficiency at warehouse scale.

  1. Significance: An optical circuit bypasses packet-by-packet electronic processing in the switching path. The complete system still consumes power for actuators, control, and transceivers. Its compatibility across line rates depends on optical loss, wavelengths, and endpoint budgets, so protocol transparency does not imply unrestricted upgrades.
  2. Distinction: Unlike electrical packet switches (which inspect headers, buffer packets in SRAM, and make nanosecond routing decisions per packet), an OCS establishes semi-static physical optical circuits with millisecond-scale switching latency (\(10\)–\(30\) ms), making it suitable for topology-level reconfiguration rather than dynamic packet routing.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming OCS replaces packet switching entirely. OCS operates at circuit granularity: it configures cluster-scale topologies (such as 3D tori or job-specific sub-clusters) and routes around hardware faults, while packet switching or accelerator-native interconnects handle fine-grained, intra-step message routing.

Google’s TPU v4 supercomputer (Jouppi et al. 2023) demonstrated the architectural power of optical circuit switching in production. Instead of wiring 4,096 chips into an inflexible electrical fat-tree, the system groups accelerators into \(4 \times 4 \times 4\) node cubes and routes the inter-cube links through an array of 3D MEMS optical circuit switches (codenamed Palomar).

Jouppi, Norm, George Kurian, Sheng Li, Peter Ma, Rahul Nagarajan, Lifeng Nai, Nishant Patil, et al. 2023. “TPU v4: An Optically Reconfigurable Supercomputer for Machine Learning with Hardware Support for Embeddings.” Proceedings of the 50th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 1–14. https://doi.org/10.1145/3579371.3589350.

This reconfigurable optical architecture delivers two fundamental operational capabilities: topology tailoring and dynamic fault recovery with boundary wrapping.

For workload adaptation, the physical interconnect can be reconfigured dynamically to match the communication structure of the running model. A training run using 1D pipeline parallelism can configure its slice as an elongated cylinder to streamline stage-to-stage transfers. Conversely, a 2D or 3D tensor-parallel partition can configure a compact cubic 3D torus that minimizes average hop count, while an MoE workload can maximize bisection bandwidth across specific expert groups.

Optical switching also supports fault isolation by changing connections around unavailable hardware where spare connectivity permits. The capability matters because in a statically cabled torus, a single failed link or node can break a ring along an entire dimension and degrade every job that spans it. With optical circuit switching, the mirrors can be redirected to bypass the failed cube or fiber and splice healthy cubes into a complete, smaller torus slice without manual recabling. The manager must first detect the fault, choose a feasible allocation, and establish the new circuits. Circuit-switching time is only one part of that process. A training job may also need to rebuild its process groups and restore state before computation resumes, so optical reconfiguration latency alone does not specify recovery time.

The trade-off is switching latency. Because MEMS mirrors physically rotate to steer collimated light beams, reconfiguration occurs on a mechanical timescale that is much longer than packet forwarding in an established electrical path. These are different operations: circuit setup changes connectivity, while packet forwarding uses connectivity already in place. Consequently, OCS moves the power and protocol wall while leaving reconfiguration latency as the binding discipline: it is deployed for job-level topology allocation and fault isolation, not for per-message dynamic routing.

The common theme is relocation rather than elimination. CXL moves capacity outward into a fabric and leaves bandwidth as the limiting discipline. Wafer-scale integration moves communication inward and leaves capacity and yield as the limiting disciplines. Advanced packaging compresses die-to-die distance and requires power density to be checked against the package and cooling budgets. Optical circuit switching removes the OEO conversion inside the switch and leaves reconfiguration latency as the limiting discipline. Infrastructure planning therefore still begins by identifying where the next byte moves, how quickly it must arrive, and what physical system must be built so that movement does not dominate the workload.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A representative CXL memory path over PCIe Gen5 x16 delivers roughly \(64\text{ GB/s}\) of bandwidth, approximately \(50\times\) lower than an H100’s \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\) HBM3 bandwidth. Which infrastructure bottleneck is CXL memory pooling designed to relieve, and which bottleneck does it explicitly not solve?

    1. It relieves peak Tensor Core throughput shortages, but does not solve the memory capacity limit.
    2. It relieves rack cooling limits by eliminating electrical resistance, but does not solve chip-to-chip latency.
    3. It relieves the memory capacity ceiling by holding phase-local optimizer state in shared pooled memory, but explicitly does not replace HBM for bandwidth-critical forward and backward pass weight streaming.
    4. It relieves wafer-scale weight injection bottlenecks, but does not solve semiconductor yield.

  2. A systems team proposes moving a 1.8-trillion parameter LLM training workload to a Wafer-Scale Engine (such as Cerebras WSE-3) to eliminate chip-to-chip network communication delays. Explain why this move changes rather than eliminates communication constraints and identify the resulting capacity and bandwidth budgets.

  3. Advanced packaging technologies (such as 2.5D silicon interposers and 3D chiplet stacking) enable dense die-to-die interconnects on a single package. According to systems engineering principles, how does advanced packaging alter the landscape of infrastructure bottlenecks?

    1. It allows accelerators to run uncooled at ambient room temperature by eliminating electrical resistance across die boundaries.
    2. It permanently eliminates both the memory wall and the power wall by integrating optical waveguides directly onto the silicon substrate.
    3. It eliminates the need for distributed training frameworks because all model parameters can fit permanently in on-die SRAM caches.
    4. It shortens die-to-die paths while requiring package power density, current transients, and heat removal to be checked against the intended configuration.

  4. True or False: Emerging infrastructure technologies such as CXL memory pooling, wafer-scale integration, and 2.5D packaging eliminate physical infrastructure constraints, allowing data center operators to deploy dense ML clusters without modifying cooling or power delivery.
